Problem 2. Compute the following sum:
                            1   1                 1
                              +    + ··· +                  .
                           1·4 4·7         (3n − 2)(3n + 1)

Prove that your answer is true for all n ∈ N using induction.

Problem 3. Let f : A → B be a function.

 (a) Prove that f is injective if and only if f −1 (f (X)) = X for all X ⊆ A.
 (b) Prove that f is surjective if and only if f (f −1 (Y )) = Y for all Y ⊆ B.

Problem 4. Let f : A → B be a function. Prove that the following two statements are
equivalent:

 (a) The function f is surjective.
 (b) For any set C and for any functions g : B → C and h : B → C such that g ◦ f = h ◦ f ,
     we have g = h.

Problem 5. Let f : B → C be a function. Prove that the following two statements are
equivalent:

 (a) The function f is injective.
 (b) For any set A and for any functions g : A → B and h : A → B such that f ◦ g = f ◦ h,
     we have g = h.

Problem 6. Let f : A → B be a function. Suppose that there exists a function g : B → A
such that (g ◦ f )(a) = a for all a ∈ A and (f ◦ g)(b) = b for all b ∈ B. Prove that f is
bijective.

Problem 1
Using induction, prove that for all n ∈ N we have

                              3 + 11 + · · · + (8n − 5) = 4n2 − n.

Proof.
For n ∈ N, let P (n) denote the statement

                              3 + 11 + · · · + (8n − 5) = 4n2 − n.

We argue by induction.
Base case. For n = 1, the left-hand side is 3, while the right-hand side is 4 · 12 − 1 = 3.
Hence P (1) holds.
Inductive step. Assume P (n) holds for some n ∈ N, i.e.

                              3 + 11 + · · · + (8n − 5) = 4n2 − n.

We must show that P (n + 1) holds. Observe that the next term in the sum is

                                    8(n + 1) − 5 = 8n + 3.

Adding this term to both sides of the inductive hypothesis gives

                 3 + 11 + · · · + (8n − 5) + (8n + 3) = (4n2 − n) + (8n + 3).

The left-hand side is exactly the sum 3 + 11 + · · · + (8(n + 1) − 5), and the right-hand side
simplifies to
                            (4n2 − n) + (8n + 3) = 4n2 + 7n + 3.
On the other hand,

               4(n + 1)2 − (n + 1) = 4(n2 + 2n + 1) − n − 1 = 4n2 + 7n + 3.

Therefore
                     3 + 11 + · · · + (8(n + 1) − 5) = 4(n + 1)2 − (n + 1),
so P (n+1) holds. By the Principle of Mathematical Induction, P (n) is true for all n ∈ N.

Problem 2
Compute the following sum:
                              1   1                 1
                                +    + ··· +                  .
                             1·4 4·7         (3n − 2)(3n + 1)

Prove that your answer is true for all n ∈ N using induction.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     We first compute the sum in closed form. For k ∈ N,
                                     1             A      B
                                               =       +       .
                              (3k − 2)(3k + 1)   3k − 2 3k + 1

     Solving
                                    1 = A(3k + 1) + B(3k − 2)
     gives A = 31 and B = − 31 . Hence
                                                              
                                   1           1   1      1
                                             =         −         .
                            (3k − 2)(3k + 1)   3 3k − 2 3k + 1

     Therefore the partial sums telescope:
                      n                                             
                      X            1           1              1                n
                                             =         1−                =          .
                            (3k − 2)(3k + 1)   3            3n + 1           3n + 1
                      k=1

     We now prove this identity by induction.

Proof.
For n ∈ N, let P (n) denote the statement
                       1   1                 1             n
                         +    + ··· +                  =        .
                      1·4 4·7         (3n − 2)(3n + 1)   3n + 1

We will prove that P (n) is true for all n ∈ N using induction.
Base case. P (1) is the statement
                                          1      1
                                             =       ,
                                         1·4   3·1+1
which is true.
Inductive step. Assume P (n) is true for some n ∈ N, that is,
                       1   1                 1             n
                         +    + ··· +                  =        .
                      1·4 4·7         (3n − 2)(3n + 1)   3n + 1

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                Homework 1


                              1
Adding the next term                    to both sides, we obtain
                       (3n + 1)(3n + 4)
         1                 1                1             n           1
            + ··· +                 +                 =       +                 .
        1·4         (3n − 2)(3n + 1) (3n + 1)(3n + 4)   3n + 1 (3n + 1)(3n + 4)

The left-hand side is exactly the sum in P (n + 1). On the right-hand side,
                                                         
          n              1              1            1           1     n(3n + 4) + 1
              +                    =          n+            =        ·               .
       3n + 1 (3n + 1)(3n + 4)       3n + 1        3n + 4     3n + 1      3n + 4

Since
                      n(3n + 4) + 1 = 3n2 + 4n + 1 = (3n + 1)(n + 1),
we obtain
                      n           1            n+1        n+1
                          +                 =        =              .
                    3n + 1 (3n + 1)(3n + 4)   3n + 4   3(n + 1) + 1
Thus P (n + 1) is true. By the Principle of Mathematical Induction, P (n) is true for all
n ∈ N.

Problem 3
Let f : A → B be a function.

(a) Prove that f is injective if and only if f −1 (f (X)) = X for all X ⊆ A.
(b) Prove that f is surjective if and only if f f −1 (Y ) = Y for all Y ⊆ B.
                                                         


Discussion & Scratch Work

     Key facts (true for any function f : A → B):

             X ⊆ f −1 (f (X))                        f f −1 (Y ) ⊆ Y
                                                                
                                for all X ⊆ A,                         for all Y ⊆ B.

     The nontrivial directions use injectivity/surjectivity.

Proof.
Let f : A → B be a function.

(a) (⇒) Assume f is injective. Let X ⊆ A. We prove f −1 (f (X)) = X by double inclusion.
    First, if x ∈ X, then f (x) ∈ f (X), so by definition of preimage, x ∈ f −1 (f (X)). Hence
    X ⊆ f −1 (f (X)).
    Conversely, let a ∈ f −1 (f (X)). Then f (a) ∈ f (X), so there exists x ∈ X with f (a) =
    f (x). Since f is injective, a = x ∈ X. Thus f −1 (f (X)) ⊆ X.
    Therefore f −1 (f (X)) = X.
    (⇐) Assume that for all X ⊆ A we have f −1 (f (X)) = X. To show f is injective, let
    a1 , a2 ∈ A and suppose f (a1 ) = f (a2 ). Set X = {a1 }. Then f (a2 ) = f (a1 ) ∈ f (X), so
    a2 ∈ f −1 (f (X)) = X = {a1 }. Hence a2 = a1 . Therefore f is injective.
(b) (⇒) Assume f is surjective. Let Y ⊆ B. We prove f (f −1 (Y )) = Y by double inclusion.
    First, if b ∈ f (f −1 (Y )), then by definition there exists a ∈ f −1 (Y ) with f (a) = b. But
    a ∈ f −1 (Y ) means f (a) ∈ Y , so b ∈ Y . Hence f (f −1 (Y )) ⊆ Y .
    Conversely, let y ∈ Y . Since f is surjective, there exists a ∈ A such that f (a) = y.
    Then f (a) = y ∈ Y , so a ∈ f −1 (Y ), and therefore y = f (a) ∈ f (f −1 (Y )). Thus
    Y ⊆ f (f −1 (Y )).
    Therefore f (f −1 (Y )) = Y .
    (⇐) Assume that for all Y ⊆ B we have f (f −1 (Y )) = Y . Take Y = B. Then

                                           f (f −1 (B)) = B.

    But f −1 (B) = A (every a ∈ A satisfies f (a) ∈ B), so this reads f (A) = B. Hence f is
    surjective.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                     Homework 1


Problem 4
Let f : A → B be a function. Prove that the following two statements are equivalent:

(a) The function f is surjective.
(b) For any set C and for any functions g : B → C and h : B → C such that g ◦ f = h ◦ f ,
    we have g = h.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     This is the “right-cancellation” property: f is surjective iff precomposition with f de-
     tects equality of functions B → C. The forward direction uses surjectivity to pull back
     an arbitrary b ∈ B to some a ∈ A. The reverse direction is proved by contradiction:
     if f is not surjective, choose b0 ∈ B \ f (A) and build two functions g, h : B → C that
     agree on f (A) but differ at b0 .

Proof.
Let f : A → B be a function.

(⇒) Assume that f is surjective. Let C be any set, and let g, h : B → C satisfy g ◦ f = h ◦ f .
We show that g = h.
Fix b ∈ B. Since f is surjective, there exists a ∈ A such that f (a) = b. Then

                 g(b) = g(f (a)) = (g ◦ f )(a) = (h ◦ f )(a) = h(f (a)) = h(b).

Since b ∈ B was arbitrary, we conclude g = h.

(⇐) Assume that for every set C and all functions g, h : B → C,

                                 g◦f =h◦f            =⇒   g = h.

We prove that f is surjective. Suppose, for contradiction, that f is not surjective. Then
there exists b0 ∈ B \ f (A).
Let C = {0, 1}, and define g, h : B → C by
                                                             (
                                                              1, b = b0 ,
                       g(b) = 0 for all b ∈ B,        h(b) =
                                                              0, b ̸= b0 .

Then g ̸= h (since g(b0 ) = 0 but h(b0 ) = 1). However, for every a ∈ A we have f (a) ̸= b0
(because b0 ∈
            / f (A)), so h(f (a)) = 0 = g(f (a)). Hence

              (g ◦ f )(a) = g(f (a)) = 0 = h(f (a)) = (h ◦ f )(a) for all a ∈ A,

which implies g ◦f = h◦f . By the assumed property, this would force g = h, a contradiction.
Therefore f must be surjective.

Problem 5
Let f : B → C be a function. Prove that the following two statements are equivalent:

(a) The function f is injective.
(b) For any set A and for any functions g : A → B and h : A → B such that f ◦ g = f ◦ h,
    we have g = h.

Discussion & Scratch Work

      This is the “left-cancellation” property: f is injective iff postcomposition with f detects
      equality of functions A → B. The forward direction uses injectivity to cancel f from
      the equation f ◦g = f ◦h pointwise. The reverse direction is proved by contradiction: if
      f is not injective, pick b1 ̸= b2 with f (b1 ) = f (b2 ) and build two functions g, h : A → B
      that differ at one point but become equal after applying f .

Proof.
Let f : B → C be a function.

(⇒) Assume that f is injective. Let A be any set, and let g, h : A → B satisfy f ◦ g = f ◦ h.
We show that g = h.
Fix a ∈ A. Then
                           f (g(a)) = (f ◦ g)(a) = (f ◦ h)(a) = f (h(a)).
Since f is injective, this implies g(a) = h(a). Because a ∈ A was arbitrary, we conclude
g = h.

(⇐) Assume that for every set A and all functions g, h : A → B,

                                     f ◦g =f ◦h         =⇒    g = h.

We prove that f is injective. Suppose, for contradiction, that f is not injective. Then there
exist b1 , b2 ∈ B with b1 ̸= b2 and f (b1 ) = f (b2 ).
Let A = {0, 1} and define g, h : A → B by

                       g(0) = b1 ,    g(1) = b1 ,       h(0) = b1 ,    h(1) = b2 .

Then g ̸= h (since g(1) = b1 ̸= b2 = h(1)). However, for each a ∈ A we have
                           (                                            (
                              f (b1 ), a = 0,                            f (b1 ), a = 0,
   (f ◦ g)(a) = f (g(a)) =                      (f ◦ h)(a) = f (h(a)) =
                              f (b1 ), a = 1,                            f (b2 ), a = 1,

and since f (b1 ) = f (b2 ), it follows that (f ◦g)(a) = (f ◦h)(a) for all a ∈ A. Thus f ◦g = f ◦h.
By the assumed property, this would force g = h, a contradiction. Therefore f must be
injective.

Problem 6
Let f : A → B be a function. Suppose that there exists a function g : B → A such that
(g ◦ f )(a) = a for all a ∈ A and (f ◦ g)(b) = b for all b ∈ B. Prove that f is bijective.
Discussion & Scratch Work

      The hypotheses say that g undoes the action of f on both sides:

                  (g ◦ f )(a) = a for all a ∈ A,       (f ◦ g)(b) = b for all b ∈ B.

      From the first identity, applying g to an equality f (a1 ) = f (a2 ) forces a1 = a2 , which
      implies injectivity. From the second identity, every b ∈ B can be written as b = f (g(b)),
      which implies surjectivity.

Proof.
Let f : A → B and suppose there exists g : B → A such that

               (g ◦ f )(a) = a for all a ∈ A,          (f ◦ g)(b) = b for all b ∈ B.

We show that f is injective and surjective.
Injective. Let a1 , a2 ∈ A and assume f (a1 ) = f (a2 ). Applying g to both sides gives

                                        g(f (a1 )) = g(f (a2 )).

But by the first hypothesis, g(f (a)) = a for any a ∈ A, so a1 = a2 . Hence f is injective.
Surjective. Let b ∈ B. Set a = g(b) ∈ A. Then

                                 f (a) = f (g(b)) = (f ◦ g)(b) = b.

Thus every b ∈ B is hit by f , so f is surjective.
Since f is both injective and surjective, f is bijective.

Appendix: Toolbox
Definition 1 (Natural Numbers). The set of natural numbers N = {1, 2, 3, . . . } is defined
axiomatically by the Peano axioms:

(a) 1 ∈ N.
(b) If n ∈ N, then n + 1 ∈ N.
(c) 1 is not the successor of any natural number.
(d) If n + 1 = m + 1, then n = m.
(e) ( Induction Axiom) If S ⊆ N satisfies 1 ∈ S and n ∈ S ⇒ n + 1 ∈ S, then S = N.

Theorem 1 (Principle of Mathematical Induction). Let P (n) be a statement defined for all
n ∈ N. If:

(a) P (1) is true (base case), and
(b) for all n ∈ N, P (n) ⇒ P (n + 1) (inductive step),

then P (n) is true for all n ∈ N.

Remark. The Principle of Mathematical Induction follows from the Peano axioms. Given
a statement P (n), define
                            S = {n ∈ N : P (n) is true}.
If 1 ∈ S and n ∈ S ⇒ n + 1 ∈ S, then S = N by the Induction Axiom.

Definition 2 (Function). Let A and B be nonempty sets. A function f : A → B assigns to
each element a ∈ A exactly one element f (a) ∈ B. The set A is called the domain and the
set B the codomain.

Definition 3 (Composition of Functions). Let f : A → B and g : B → C be functions. The
composition of g with f is the function g ◦ f : A → C defined by

                             (g ◦ f )(a) = g(f (a)) for all a ∈ A.

Definition 4 (Image and Preimage). Let f : A → B be a function.

 • For X ⊆ A, the image of X under f is

                                       f (X) = {f (x) : x ∈ X}.

 • For Y ⊆ B, the preimage of Y under f is

                                    f −1 (Y ) = {a ∈ A : f (a) ∈ Y }.

Definition 5 (Injective, Surjective, Bijective). Let f : A → B be a function.

                                                 9
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                 Homework 1


 • f is injective if for all a1 , a2 ∈ A,

                                      f (a1 ) = f (a2 ) ⇒ a1 = a2 .

 • f is surjective if f (A) = B, i.e. for every b ∈ B there exists a ∈ A such that f (a) = b.
 • f is bijective if it is both injective and surjective.

Remark. For a general function f : A → B, the notation f −1 (Y ) denotes a preimage and
does not require f to be invertible. Moreover, the properties of being injective, surjective,
or bijective depend not only on the rule defining f , but also on the choice of domain A and
codomain B.

Problem 1. Prove that if A and B are both countable sets and A ∩ B = ∅, then A ∪ B is
countable. (Hint: We proved that Z is countable in class. Try using a similar idea here.)

Problem 2. Let An be a countable set for each n ∈ N, and suppose that Am ∩ An = ∅
whenever m 6= n. Prove that the union A = A1 ∪ A2 ∪ A3 ∪ . . . is countable. (Hint: We
proved that N × N is countable in class. Try using a similar idea here.)

Problem 3. Recall that given two sets A and B, we write A ∼ B if and only if there exists
a bijective function f : A → B. Prove that ∼ is an equivalence relation.

Problem 4. For a, b ∈ Z, define a ∼ b if and only if b − a is divisible by 2.

 (a) Prove that ∼ is an equivalence relation on Z.
 (b) Find the equivalence classes.

Problem 5. Let (F, +, ·) be a field.

 (a) Prove that for any x ∈ F , the function f : F → F , f (y) = x + y is bijective.
 (b) Prove that for any x ∈ F \ {0}, the function g : F → F , g(y) = x · y is bijective.

Problem 6. Let (F, +, ·) be a field. Suppose that F = {0, 1, a, b} is a set with exactly
four elements, where 0 and 1 denote the identities for + and · respectively, and a, b denote
the remaining two elements of F . Fill in the addition and multiplication tables below, and
justify your answer.
                        + 0 1 a b                    ·   0 1 a b
                        0                            0
                        1                            1
                        a                            a
                        b                            b

(Hint: Using the previous problem, show that each row and column in the addition table
contains every element of F exactly once, like a Sudoku puzzle. Show that the same is true
for the nonzero rows and columns of the multiplication table. Note that for each entry, there
is only one correct answer.)

Problem 1
Prove that if A and B are both countable sets and A ∩ B = ∅, then A ∪ B is countable.
(Hint: We proved that Z is countable in class. Try using a similar idea here.)
Discussion & Scratch Work

     Because A and B are countable, there exist enumerations

                         A = {a1 , a2 , a3 , . . . },       B = {b1 , b2 , b3 , . . . }.

     Intuitively, this means we can list the elements of A and B in a sequence. To show
     that A ∪ B is countable, we want to construct a single list that contains every element
     of A and B exactly once. The idea is to interleave the two lists, alternating between
     an element of A and an element of B. The assumption A ∩ B = ∅ ensures that no
     element appears in both lists, so this alternating procedure never produces duplicates.

Proof.
Since A and B are countable, there exist bijections

                                  f : N → A and g : N → B.

Write an = f (n) and bn = g(n), so that

                      A = {a1 , a2 , a3 , . . . } and B = {b1 , b2 , b3 , . . . }.

Define a function h : N → A ∪ B by

                       h(2n − 1) = an         and h(2n) = bn                   (n ∈ N).

We show that h is bijective.
First, h is surjective. Let x ∈ A ∪ B. If x ∈ A, then x = an for some n, and hence
x = h(2n − 1). If x ∈ B, then x = bn for some n, and hence x = h(2n). Thus every element
of A ∪ B is in the image of h.
Next, h is injective. Suppose that h(k) = h(ℓ). If k and ℓ have different parity, then one of
h(k) lies in A and the other lies in B, which is impossible because A ∩ B = ∅. Hence k and
ℓ must have the same parity. If k = 2m − 1 and ℓ = 2n − 1, then am = an , and since f is
injective, we have m = n, so k = ℓ. If k = 2m and ℓ = 2n, then bm = bn , and since g is
injective, we again obtain m = n, so k = ℓ.
Therefore h is a bijection from N onto A ∪ B. It follows that A ∪ B is countable.

Problem 2
Let An be a countable set for each n ∈ N, and suppose that Am ∩ An = ∅ whenever m ̸= n.
Prove that the union A = A1 ∪ A2 ∪ A3 ∪ · · · is countable. (Hint: We proved that N × N is
countable in class. Try using a similar idea here.)
Discussion & Scratch Work

     Because each An is countable, we can “list” its elements as

                                   An = {an,1 , an,2 , an,3 , . . . }.
                                            S
     So every element of the big union A = n∈N An comes with two pieces of information:
     (1) which set An it belongs to, and (2) its position in the list of An .
     That suggests encoding an element of A by a pair (n, k) ∈ N × N, where n tells us
     which An we are in, and k tells us which element of An we chose. Since we proved in
     class that N × N is countable, we can list all pairs (n, k) in a single sequence. If we
     map (n, k) to the k-th element of An , we get a single list that hits every element of A.
     The disjointness assumption Am ∩ An = ∅ for m ̸= n is what prevents duplicates from
     coming from different An ’s.

Proof.
For each n ∈ N, the set An is countable, so there exists a bijection
                                          fn : N → An .
Define a function h : N × N → A by
                            h(n, k) := fn (k)       ((n, k) ∈ N × N).
We claim that h is bijective.
                                                  S
Surjectivity. Let x ∈ A. By definition of A = n∈N An , there exists some n ∈ N such that
x ∈ An . Since fn is surjective onto An , there exists k ∈ N with fn (k) = x. Thus h(n, k) = x,
so h is surjective.
Injectivity. Suppose h(n, k) = h(m, ℓ). Then
                                         fn (k) = fm (ℓ).
The left-hand side lies in An and the right-hand side lies in Am . If n ̸= m, then this would
imply An ∩ Am ̸= ∅, contradicting the hypothesis that the An are pairwise disjoint. Hence
n = m. With n = m, we have fn (k) = fn (ℓ), and since fn is injective, it follows that k = ℓ.
Therefore (n, k) = (m, ℓ), and h is injective.
So h is a bijection N × N → A.
Finally, we proved in class that N × N is countable, i.e. there exists a bijection
                                        φ : N → N × N.
Then the composition h ◦ φ : N → A is a bijection. Therefore A is countable.

Problem 3
Recall that given two sets A and B, we write A ∼ B if and only if there exists a bijective
function f : A → B. Prove that ∼ is an equivalence relation.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     We want to prove that ∼ is an equivalence relation, so we must check three properties:
     reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity.
     The definition says A ∼ B means “there exists a bijection f : A → B.”

      • Reflexive: any set A is in bijection with itself via the identity function defined by
        f : A → A with f (a) = a for all a ∈ A.
      • Symmetric: if A ∼ B, there is a bijection f : A → B. A bijection has an inverse
        function f −1 : B → A, and f −1 is also a bijection, so B ∼ A.
      • Transitive: if A ∼ B and B ∼ C, then there are bijections f : A → B and
        g : B → C. Their composition g ◦ f : A → C is again a bijection, so A ∼ C.

     So the whole proof is basically: identity gives reflexive, inverse gives symmetric, and
     composition gives transitive.

Proof.
We verify the three defining properties.
(Reflexivity). Let A be a set. Define a function f : A → A by f (a) = a for all a ∈ A. Then
f is bijective. Hence A ∼ A.
(Symmetry). Suppose A ∼ B. Then there exists a bijection f : A → B. Since f is bijective,
it has an inverse function f −1 : B → A, and f −1 is bijective. Therefore B ∼ A.
(Transitivity). Suppose A ∼ B and B ∼ C. Then there exist bijections f : A → B and
g : B → C. The composition g ◦ f : A → C is bijective, so A ∼ C.
Thus ∼ is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive; hence it is an equivalence relation.




                                                4
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                      Homework 2


Problem 4
For a, b ∈ Z, define a ∼ b if and only if b − a is divisible by 2.

(a) Prove that ∼ is an equivalence relation on Z.
(b) Find the equivalence classes.

Discussion & Scratch Work

      Big picture. The relation is saying that two integers are equivalent when they have
      the same parity (both even or both odd). Another way to say this is that a ∼ b exactly
      when a and b differ by an even integer.
      To prove that ∼ is an equivalence relation, we check the three properties from the
      notes:

       • Reflexive: always true because a − a = 0 is divisible by 2.
       • Symmetric: if b − a is divisible by 2, then a − b = −(b − a) is also divisible by 2.
       • Transitive: if b − a and c − b are divisible by 2, then adding them gives c − a =
         (c − b) + (b − a), also divisible by 2.

      For part (b), once you realize this is “same parity,” there should be exactly two equiv-
      alence classes: the evens and the odds. Concretely:

      [0] = {even integers} = {2k : k ∈ Z} = 2Z,       [1] = {odd integers} = {2k+1 : k ∈ Z} = 2Z+1.

      Any even integer is equivalent to 0, and any odd integer is equivalent to 1.

Proof.
(a) We show that ∼ is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive on Z.
Reflexive. Let a ∈ Z. Then a − a = 0, and 0 is divisible by 2. Hence a ∼ a.
Symmetric. Suppose a ∼ b. By definition, b − a is divisible by 2, so b − a = 2k for some
k ∈ Z. Then
                         a − b = −(b − a) = −2k = 2(−k),
so a − b is divisible by 2. Thus b ∼ a.
Transitive. Suppose a ∼ b and b ∼ c. Then b − a = 2k and c − b = 2ℓ for some k, ℓ ∈ Z.
Adding,
                     c − a = (c − b) + (b − a) = 2ℓ + 2k = 2(k + ℓ),
which is divisible by 2. Hence a ∼ c.
Therefore ∼ is an equivalence relation on Z.
(b) The equivalence classes are exactly the parity classes. First, if n ∈ Z is even, then
n − 0 = n is divisible by 2, so n ∼ 0; hence every even integer lies in [0]. Conversely, if
n ∈ [0], then n ∼ 0, so n − 0 = n is divisible by 2, which means n is even. Thus

                                    [0] = {2k : k ∈ Z} = 2Z.

                                                   5
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                 Homework 2


Similarly, if n ∈ Z is odd then n − 1 is even (hence divisible by 2), so n ∼ 1 and n ∈ [1]; and
if n ∈ [1], then n ∼ 1 so n − 1 is divisible by 2, meaning n is odd. Hence

                              [1] = {2k + 1 : k ∈ Z} = 2Z + 1.

These are the two equivalence classes.




                                              6
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                     Homework 2


Problem 5
Let (F, +, ·) be a field.

(a) Prove that for any x ∈ F , the function f : F → F , f (y) = x + y is bijective.
(b) Prove that for any x ∈ F \ {0}, the function g : F → F , g(y) = x · y is bijective.

Discussion & Scratch Work

      Big picture. In a field, “adding a fixed element” and “multiplying by a fixed nonzero
      element” are reversible operations.

       • For (a), if we define f (y) = x + y, then we can undo it by adding the additive
         inverse −x:
                               f (y) = x + y =⇒ y = (−x) + f (y).
         So the inverse candidate is F → F , y 7→ (−x) + y.
       • For (b), if x ̸= 0 and we define g(y) = x · y, then we can undo it by multiplying by
         x−1 :
                                  g(y) = x · y =⇒ y = x−1 · g(y).
         So the inverse candidate is F → F , y 7→ x−1 · y.

      Once we exhibit an inverse function (and verify the two composition identities), bijec-
      tivity follows.

Proof.
(a) Fix x ∈ F and define f : F → F by f (y) = x + y. Define h : F → F by

                                        h(y) = (−x) + y.

We show that h is the inverse of f by checking both compositions.
For any y ∈ F ,
                                                                  
     (h ◦ f )(y) = h(f (y)) = h(x + y) = (−x) + (x + y) = (−x) + x + y = 0 + y = y,

using associativity of +, the definition of −x, and the additive identity.
For any y ∈ F ,

   (f ◦ h)(y) = f (h(y)) = f ((−x) + y) = x + ((−x) + y) = (x + (−x)) + y = 0 + y = y,

again by associativity and the definition of additive inverse.
Thus h = f −1 , so f is bijective.

(b) Fix x ∈ F \ {0} and define g : F → F by g(y) = x · y. Since x ̸= 0, there exists x−1 ∈ F
such that x · x−1 = x−1 · x = 1. Define k : F → F by

                                         k(y) = x−1 · y.

                                                7
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                     Homework 2


We again check both compositions. For any y ∈ F ,

           (k ◦ g)(y) = k(g(y)) = k(x · y) = x−1 · (x · y) = (x−1 · x) · y = 1 · y = y,

using associativity of · and the multiplicative identity.
For any y ∈ F ,

          (g ◦ k)(y) = g(k(y)) = g(x−1 · y) = x · (x−1 · y) = (x · x−1 ) · y = 1 · y = y.

Thus k = g −1 , so g is bijective.




                                                8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                       Homework 2


Problem 6
Let (F, +, ·) be a field. Suppose that F = {0, 1, a, b} is a set with exactly four elements,
where 0 and 1 denote the identities for + and · respectively, and a, b denote the remaining
two elements of F . Fill in the addition and multiplication tables below, and justify your
answer.
                             + 0 1 a b           · 0 1 a b
                             0                   0
                             1                   1
                             a                   a
                             b                   b
(Hint: Using the previous problem, show that each row and column in the addition table
contains every element of F exactly once, like a Sudoku puzzle. Show that the same is true
for the nonzero rows and columns of the multiplication table. Note that for each entry, there
is only one correct answer.)
Discussion & Scratch Work

     Strategy. We know F = {0, 1, a, b} is a field, so (F, +) is an abelian group of order 4
     and F \ {0} = {1, a, b} is an abelian group under · of order 3.

     Addition table. Since |F | = 4 = 22 , the characteristic of F must be 2 (the charac-
     teristic of a field is a prime p and |F | is a power of p). Hence

                                            1 + 1 = 0.

     In fact, in an additive group of order 4 with characteristic 2, every element satisfies
     x + x = 0. So
                                   1 + 1 = a + a = b + b = 0.
     Now use the “Sudoku” constraint: for each fixed x ∈ F , the map y 7→ x + y is a
     bijection (Problem 5 style), so each row/column of the addition table contains each
     element of F exactly once. For example, in the row for 1, the entries are 1 + 0 = 1 and
     1 + 1 = 0, so the remaining two entries must be a and b. Also 1 + a cannot be 0 (else
     a = −1 = 1) and cannot be 1 (else a = 0) and cannot be a (else 1 = 0), so necessarily
     1 + a = b. Similarly 1 + b = a, and then a + b = 1.

     Multiplication table. The nonzero elements form a group of order 3, so it is cyclic.
     In particular, for any x ∈ {a, b}, we have x3 = 1 and x ̸= 1. Thus if we pick a ̸= 1, then
     a2 ̸= 1 and must equal the remaining nonzero element b. Then a3 = a · a2 = a · b = 1, so
     ab = 1. Commutativity gives ba = 1. Also b = a2 implies b2 = a4 = a (since a3 = 1),
     and the row/column “Sudoku” constraint for multiplication by a nonzero element gives
     that each nonzero row/column is a permutation of {1, a, b}. Finally, 0 times anything
     is 0.

Proof.
We determine the operation tables using only field axioms and the hint.


                                                 9
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                   Homework 2


Step 1: the addition table. Since F is a field with |F | = 4, its characteristic is a prime
p and |F | = pn for some n ∈ N. Hence 4 = pn forces p = 2. Therefore
                                             1 + 1 = 0.
More generally, for any x ∈ F we have x + x = (1 + 1)x = 0 · x = 0, so
                                  1 + 1 = a + a = b + b = 0.
Now fix any x ∈ F . By Problem 5(a) (applied in the additive group), the map Tx : F → F
given by Tx (y) = x+y is bijective. Hence each row and column of the addition table contains
every element of F exactly once.
We already know the 0-row and 0-column are identities: 0 + y = y and x + 0 = x. In the
1-row we have 1 + 0 = 1 and 1 + 1 = 0, so 1 + a and 1 + b must be the remaining two elements
a and b. But 1 + a cannot equal 0, 1, or a (each would force a contradiction), so 1 + a = b.
Then the remaining entry is 1 + b = a. Finally, using commutativity, a + b = b + a, and
using 1 + a = b we get
a+b = 1 (since adding a to both sides of 1 + a = b gives 1 + (a + a) = b + a, i.e. 1 = b + a).
Thus the addition table is
                                        +     0   1    a   b
                                        0     0   1    a   b
                                        1     1   0    b   a
                                        a     a   b    0   1
                                        b     b   a    1   0

Step 2: the multiplication table. The set F \ {0} = {1, a, b} is an abelian group under
· of order 3, hence cyclic. So the two elements a and b are the two non-identity elements of
this cyclic group. In particular, a ̸= 1 and a3 = 1. Since a2 ̸= 1 (otherwise a would have
order 2, impossible in a group of order 3), we must have
                                              a2 = b.
Then
                                    a · b = a · a2 = a3 = 1,
so ab = 1. By commutativity, ba = 1. Also b = a2 implies
                                     b2 = a4 = a · a3 = a.
As usual in a field, 0 · x = 0 for all x ∈ F . Therefore the multiplication table is
                                         ·    0   1    a   b
                                         0    0   0    0   0
                                         1    0   1    a   b
                                         a    0   a    b   1
                                         b    0   b    1   a

These tables are uniquely determined by the field axioms and the “Sudoku” constraints in
the hint.

Appendix: Toolbox
Week 1 Recap: Induction and Functions
Natural numbers and induction. We use
                                            N = {1, 2, 3, . . . }.
Principle of Mathematical Induction. Let P (n) be a statement defined for all n ∈ N.
If

(a) P (1) is true, and
(b) for all n ∈ N, P (n) ⇒ P (n + 1),

then P (n) is true for all n ∈ N.

Functions. Let A, B be nonempty sets. A function f : A → B assigns to each a ∈ A
exactly one element f (a) ∈ B. The set A is the domain and B is the codomain.

Composition. If f : A → B and g : B → C are functions, their composition is the
function g ◦ f : A → C given by
                                 (g ◦ f )(a) = g(f (a))          (a ∈ A).

Image and preimage. Let f : A → B.

 • For X ⊆ A, the image of X under f is
                                        f (X) = {f (x) : x ∈ X}.

 • For Y ⊆ B, the preimage of Y under f is
                                    f −1 (Y ) = {a ∈ A : f (a) ∈ Y }.

Remark. For a general function f : A → B, the notation f −1 (Y ) denotes a preimage and
does not require f to be invertible.

Injective / Surjective / Bijective. Let f : A → B.

 • f is injective if for all a1 , a2 ∈ A,
                                       f (a1 ) = f (a2 ) ⇒ a1 = a2 .

 • f is surjective if f (A) = B, i.e. for every b ∈ B there exists a ∈ A such that f (a) = b.
 • f is bijective if it is both injective and surjective.

Remark. These properties depend on both the rule defining f and the choice of domain A
and codomain B.

                                                     11
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                    Homework 2


Countability
Finite and countable. A set S is finite if S = ∅ or there exists n ∈ N and a bijection
{1, 2, . . . , n} → S. A set S is countably infinite if there exists a bijection N → S. A set S is
countable if it is finite or countably infinite.

Equivalent characterizations. The following are standard and often useful:

 • S is countable ⇐⇒ there exists an injection S ,→ N.
 • S is countable ⇐⇒ there exists a surjection N ↠ S.

Countability of N × N. There are many explicit bijections N × N → N (e.g. “diagonal”
enumeration / pairing functions). In proofs, we often use the fact that

                                      N × N is countable.

Disjoint unions. If A and B are countable and A ∩ B = ∅, then A ∪ B is countable (one
can interleave enumerations, or inject A ∪ B into N × {1, 2}). More generally, if (An )n∈N are
countable and pairwise disjoint, then
                                     [
                                        An is countable
                                      n∈N

by coding an element x ∈ An as the pair (n, k) where k is its index in an enumeration of An .


Relations and Equivalence Relations
Binary relations. A (binary) relation R on a set X is a subset R ⊆ X × X. We write
x R y instead of (x, y) ∈ R.

Equivalence relation. A relation ∼ on X is an equivalence relation if it is:

 • Reflexive: x ∼ x for all x ∈ X.
 • Symmetric: x ∼ y ⇒ y ∼ x.
 • Transitive: x ∼ y and y ∼ z ⇒ x ∼ z.

Equivalence classes. If ∼ is an equivalence relation on X and x ∈ X, the equivalence
class of x is
                              [x] := {y ∈ X : y ∼ x}.
The set of all equivalence classes is denoted X/ ∼.


                                               12
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                 Homework 2


Partition property. Equivalence relations and partitions are two sides of the same coin:

 • The equivalence classes {[x] : x ∈ X} form a partition of X (classes are disjoint and their
   union is X).
 • Conversely, any partition of X defines an equivalence relation (declare two elements
   equivalent iff they lie in the same block).

Example: congruence mod 2 on Z. For a, b ∈ Z, define a ∼ b iff b − a is divisible by 2.
Then ∼ is an equivalence relation and the equivalence classes are the even and odd integers:

                                     [0] = 2Z,      [1] = 2Z + 1.


Fields
Definition (Field). A triple (F, +, ·) is a field if F is a set with at least two elements and
+ and · are operations on F satisfying:
Addition axioms.

(A1) (Closure) If a, b ∈ F , then a + b ∈ F .
(A2) (Commutativity) If a, b ∈ F , then a + b = b + a.
(A3) (Associativity) If a, b, c ∈ F , then (a + b) + c = a + (b + c).
(A4) (Identity) There exists 0 ∈ F such that a + 0 = 0 + a = a for all a ∈ F .
(A5) (Inverses) For every a ∈ F there exists −a ∈ F such that a + (−a) = (−a) + a = 0.

Multiplication axioms.

(M1) (Closure) If a, b ∈ F , then a · b ∈ F .
(M2) (Commutativity) If a, b ∈ F , then a · b = b · a.
(M3) (Associativity) If a, b, c ∈ F , then (a · b) · c = a · (b · c).
(M4) (Identity) There exists 1 ∈ F such that a · 1 = 1 · a = a for all a ∈ F .
(M5) (Inverses) For every a ∈ F \ {0} there exists a−1 ∈ F such that a · a−1 = a−1 · a = 1.

Distributivity.

  (D) For all a, b, c ∈ F , (a + b) · c = a · c + b · c.




                                                   13
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                Homework 2


Basic consequences (often used without comment). Let (F, +, ·) be a field.

 • The additive and multiplicative identities 0 and 1 are unique.
 • Additive inverses and multiplicative inverses (for a ̸= 0) are unique.
 • Additive cancellation: if a + b = a + c, then b = c.
 • Multiplicative cancellation: if a ̸= 0 and a · b = a · c, then b = c.
 • a · 0 = 0 for all a ∈ F .
 • (−a)(−b) = ab and a(−b) = −(ab) for all a, b ∈ F .

Translation and dilation maps Fix x ∈ F .

 • The map Tx : F → F defined by Tx (y) = x + y has inverse T−x .
 • If x ̸= 0, the map Mx : F → F defined by Mx (y) = x · y has inverse Mx−1 .


A Note on Finite Field Tables
In a field, (F, +) is an abelian group and (F \ {0}, ·) is an abelian group. Consequently:

 • In the addition table, each row/column is a permutation of F .
 • In the multiplication table, each nonzero row/column is a permutation of F \ {0}.

This is the “Sudoku” constraint your hint refers to.

                                       Math 521, Section 2

Problem 1. Let (F, +, ·) be a field.
 (a) Prove that the multiplicative identity is unique: If b ∈ F satisfies b · a = a · b = a for
     all a ∈ F , then b = 1.
 (b) Prove that multiplicative inverses are unique: If a ∈ F \ {0} and b ∈ F satisfy b · a =
     a · b = 1, then b = a−1 .
 (c) Prove that if a · b = a · c and a 6= 0, then b = c.
 (d) Prove that if a, b ∈ F then −a + (−b) = −(a + b).
 (e) Prove that if a, b ∈ F and a, b 6= 0, then a−1 · b−1 = (ab)−1 .
Problem 2. Given two points (x1 , y1 ) and (x2 , y2 ) in R2 = {(x, y) : x, y ∈ R}, we write
             (x1 , y1 ) < (x2 , y2 )   ⇐⇒     x1 < x2   or (x1 = x2 and y1 < y2 ).
 (a) Prove that < is an order relation on R2 .
 (b) Prove that (R2 , <) does not satisfy the least upper bound property. (Hint: Consider
     the set of all points on the y-axis.)
Problem 3. Recall that the set of complex numbers C consists of all numbers of the form
a + ib for a, b ∈ R. We can add and multiply complex numbers as follows:
 (a + ib) + (c + id) = (a + c) + i(b + d) and (a + ib) · (c + id) = (ac − bd) + i(ad + bc).
With these operations, it turns out (C, +, ·) is a field (but you do not need to prove this).
 (a) The definition of a field says that for any a + ib 6= 0 + i0, there exists c + id ∈ C so that
     (a + ib) · (c + id) = 1 + i0. What are c, d ∈ R in terms of a, b ∈ R? Prove your answer.
 (b) Prove that there is no order relation < on C that makes (C, +, ·, <) an ordered field.
     (Hint: Can i = 0 + i1 be positive? Negative? Zero?)
Problem 4. Let A ⊆ R be a nonempty and bounded set, let c ∈ R be a number, and define
                                        B = {c · a : a ∈ A}.
 (a) Prove that if c > 0, then sup B = c · sup A.
 (b) Prove that if c < 0, then sup B = c · inf A.
Problem 5. Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded sets, and define
                                   S = {a + b : a ∈ A and b ∈ B}.
Prove that sup S = sup A + sup B.
Problem 6. Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded sets. Prove or disprove the following
statements:
 (a) If a ≤ b for all a ∈ A and for all b ∈ B, then sup A ≤ inf B.
 (b) If a < b for all a ∈ A and for all b ∈ B, then sup A < inf B.

                                 1

Problem 1
Let (F, +, ·) be a field.

(a) Prove that the multiplicative identity is unique: If b ∈ F satisfies b · a = a · b = a for all
    a ∈ F , then b = 1.
(b) Prove that multiplicative inverses are unique: If a ∈ F \ {0} and b ∈ F satisfy b · a =
    a · b = 1, then b = a−1 .
(c) Prove that if a · b = a · c and a ̸= 0, then b = c.
(d) Prove that if a, b ∈ F then −a + (−b) = −(a + b).
(e) Prove that if a, b ∈ F and a, b ̸= 0, then a−1 · b−1 = (ab)−1 .

Discussion & Scratch Work

      Main ideas (and why we cannot “just divide”). In a general field, “division”
      is not a primitive operation. Instead, we justify any division-like step by introducing
      multiplicative inverses via (M5) and then using associativity (M3) and the identity
      (M4).

       • (a) Compare b · 1 computed from the hypothesis and from (M4).
       • (b) Insert 1 = a · a−1 (from (M5)) and reassociate (by (M3)) to force b = a−1 .
       • (c) Prove cancellation by multiplying a · b = a · c on the left by a−1 (existence from
         (M5)), then use (M3) and (M4).
       • (d) Show (−a) + (−b) is an additive inverse of (a + b) by checking (a + b) +
         ((−a) + (−b)) = 0 using (A2), (A3), (A4), (A5). Conclude it equals −(a + b) by
         uniqueness of additive inverses (proved from the axioms, as in lecture).
       • (e) Show a−1 · b−1 is an inverse of ab using commutativity (M2), associativity
         (M3), and (M5). Then apply (b) to conclude a−1 b−1 = (ab)−1 .

Proof.
Let (F, +, ·) be a field.

(a) Suppose b ∈ F satisfies

                                 b · a = a · b = a for all a ∈ F.

Let 1 ∈ F be the multiplicative identity whose existence is guaranteed by (M4). Setting
a = 1 in the hypothesis gives
                                        b · 1 = 1.
But by (M4) (applied with a = b), we also have b · 1 = b. Therefore b = 1, so the
multiplicative identity is unique.


                                                 2
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                Homework 3


(b) Suppose a ∈ F \ {0} and b ∈ F satisfy

                                       b · a = a · b = 1.

By the multiplicative inverse axiom (M5), there exists a−1 ∈ F such that

                                    a · a−1 = a−1 · a = 1.

We prove that b = a−1 . Using the multiplicative identity axiom (M4), we have

                                           b = b · 1.

Substituting 1 = a · a−1 from (M5) gives

                                       b = b · (a · a−1 ).

By associativity of multiplication (M3),

                                 b · (a · a−1 ) = (b · a) · a−1 .

Using the hypothesis b · a = 1, this becomes

                                   (b · a) · a−1 = 1 · a−1 .

Finally, by (M4) we have 1 · a−1 = a−1 . Therefore b = a−1 , so the multiplicative inverse of
a is unique.

(c) Suppose a, b, c ∈ F satisfy a · b = a · c and a ̸= 0. By (M5), there exists a−1 ∈ F such
that a−1 · a = 1. Multiply the equality a · b = a · c on the left by a−1 :

                                 a−1 · (a · b) = a−1 · (a · c).

By associativity (M3),
                                 (a−1 · a) · b = (a−1 · a) · c.
Using a−1 · a = 1 (from (M5)) and the identity axiom (M4), we get

                                  1·b=1·c           ⇒    b = c.

(d) We claim that −a + (−b) is the additive inverse of a + b, hence equals −(a + b). First
compute, using associativity (A3) and commutativity (A2) of +,
                                                       
        (a + b) + (−a) + (−b) = a + b + (−a) + (−b)           by (A3)
                                                        
                                 = a + b + (−b) + (−a)        by (A3) and (A2)
                                                
                                 = a + 0 + (−a) by (A5)
                                 = a + (−a) by (A4)
                                 = 0 by (A5).

                                                3
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                       Homework 3


Thus (−a) + (−b) is an additive inverse of a + b.
Uniqueness of additive inverses (from axioms). If x, y ∈ F both satisfy (a + b) + x = 0 and
(a + b) + y = 0, then adding −(a + b) to both sides and using (A3), (A4), (A5) yields x = y
(as in lecture). Therefore the additive inverse of a + b is unique, and since −(a + b) denotes
that unique inverse, we conclude

                            −a + (−b) = (−a) + (−b) = −(a + b).


(e) Assume a, b ∈ F with a ̸= 0 and b ̸= 0. By (M5), there exist a−1 , b−1 ∈ F such that
a · a−1 = 1 and b · b−1 = 1. Consider

                                         (ab) · (a−1 · b−1 ).

By associativity (M3) and commutativity (M2),

                    (ab) · (a−1 b−1 ) = a · b · a−1 · b−1 = (a · a−1 ) · (b · b−1 ).

Using (M5) and then (M4), this equals 1 · 1 = 1. Similarly,

                                        (a−1 b−1 ) · (ab) = 1.

Hence a−1 b−1 is a multiplicative inverse of ab. By part (b) (uniqueness of inverses),

                                        a−1 · b−1 = (ab)−1 .




                                                   4
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                     Homework 3


Problem 2
Given two points (x1 , y1 ) and (x2 , y2 ) in R2 = {(x, y) : x, y ∈ R}, we write

                     (x1 , y1 ) < (x2 , y2 ) ⇔ x1 < x2 or (x1 = x2 and y1 < y2 ).

(a) Prove that < is an order relation on R2 .
(b) Prove that (R2 , <) does not satisfy the least upper bound property. (Hint: Consider
    the set of all points on the y-axis.)

Discussion & Scratch Work

      Main ideas. This is the lexicographic order on R2 .

       • (a) To prove < is an order relation on R2 , verify the two defining properties:
         trichotomy and transitivity. The proofs reduce to the corresponding properties
         of the usual order on R by splitting into cases on the first coordinates.
       • (b) To show the least upper bound property fails, use the set A = {(0, y) : y ∈ R}
         (the y-axis). It is bounded above (e.g. by (1, 0)), but any upper bound must have
         first coordinate > 0, and then you can always find a strictly smaller upper bound
         by decreasing the first coordinate (e.g. replace (x, y) by (x/2, 0)). Hence no least
         upper bound exists.

Proof.
Let p = (x1 , y1 ), q = (x2 , y2 ), and r = (x3 , y3 ) be points in R2 .

(a) We prove that < is an order relation on R2 in the sense of the lecture definition.
Trichotomy. By trichotomy of the usual order on R, exactly one of x1 < x2 , x1 = x2 , or
x2 < x1 holds.

 • If x1 < x2 , then p < q by definition.
 • If x2 < x1 , then q < p by definition.
 • If x1 = x2 , then by trichotomy on R applied to y1 , y2 , exactly one of y1 < y2 , y1 = y2 , or
   y2 < y1 holds. In the first case, p < q; in the second, p = q; in the third, q < p.

Therefore exactly one of p < q, p = q, or q < p holds.
Transitivity. Assume p < q and q < r. We show p < r.
If x1 < x2 , then from q < r we have either x2 < x3 or x2 = x3 . In both cases x2 ≤ x3 , hence
x1 < x3 , so p < r by definition.
If instead x1 = x2 , then p < q forces y1 < y2 . Now consider q < r:

 • If x2 < x3 , then x1 = x2 < x3 , so p < r.

                                                    5
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                  Homework 3


 • If x2 = x3 , then q < r forces y2 < y3 , and by transitivity in R we get y1 < y3 , so (since
   x1 = x3 ) we conclude p < r.

Thus < is transitive.
Since < satisfies trichotomy and transitivity, it is an order relation on R2 .

(b) Let
                                  A := {(0, y) : y ∈ R} ⊆ R2 .
Then A is nonempty (for example (0, 0) ∈ A). We claim A is bounded above in (R2 , <).
Indeed, (1, 0) is an upper bound, because for any (0, y) ∈ A we have 0 < 1, hence (0, y) <
(1, 0) by the definition of <.

We show A has no least upper bound. First note that if (u, v) is any upper bound of A,
then necessarily u > 0. If u < 0, then (0, 0) ∈ A would satisfy (u, v) < (0, 0), so (u, v) could
not be an upper bound. If u = 0, then being an upper bound would require y ≤ v for all
y ∈ R, which is impossible since A contains all points (0, y) with y ∈ R and R is unbounded
above.

Now take any upper bound (u, v) of A; then u > 0. Define w := (u/2, 0). For any (0, y) ∈ A
we have 0 < u/2, hence (0, y) < (u/2, 0) = w, so w is also an upper bound of A. But
w < (u, v) because u/2 < u.

Therefore every upper bound of A is strictly larger than some other upper bound of A, so no
least upper bound exists. Hence (R2 , <) does not satisfy the least upper bound property.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                    Homework 3


Problem 3
Recall that the set of complex numbers C consists of all numbers of the form a + ib for
a, b ∈ R. We can add and multiply complex numbers as follows:

 (a + ib) + (c + id) = (a + c) + i(b + d) and (a + ib) · (c + id) = (ac − bd) + i(ad + bc).

With these operations, it turns out (C, +, ·) is a field (but you do not need to prove this).

(a) The definition of a field says that for any a + ib ̸= 0 + i0, there exists c + id ∈ C so that
    (a + ib) · (c + id) = 1 + i0. What are c, d ∈ R in terms of a, b ∈ R? Prove your answer.
(b) Prove that there is no order relation < on C that makes (C, +, ·, <) an ordered field.
    (Hint: Can i = 0 + i1 be positive? Negative? Zero?)

Discussion & Scratch Work

     Main ideas.

       • (a) To find (a+ib)−1 , write an unknown inverse as c+id and expand (a+ib)(c+id) =
         (ac − bd) + i(ad + bc). Matching real and imaginary parts with 1 + i0 gives a 2 × 2
         linear system. Solve it to get
                                          a                     b
                                   c=             ,       d=− 2     .
                                        a2 + b2              a + b2

         The key point is that a2 + b2 ̸= 0 whenever a + ib ̸= 0.
       • (b) In an ordered field, we always have 1 > 0 and x ̸= 0 ⇒ x2 > 0 (Appendix,
         (P3)). But in C we have i2 = −1. Trichotomy forces i > 0, i = 0, or i < 0;
         each case contradicts x2 > 0 together with 1 > 0. So no order can make C into an
         ordered field.

Proof.
(a) Let a + ib ∈ C with a + ib ̸= 0 + i0. We seek c + id ∈ C such that

                                   (a + ib)(c + id) = 1 + i0.

Using the given multiplication rule,

                           (a + ib)(c + id) = (ac − bd) + i(ad + bc).

Thus we require
                                 ac − bd = 1,             ad + bc = 0.                         (1)
Multiply the first equation in (1) by a and the second by b, then add:

                                  a(ac − bd) + b(ad + bc) = a.


                                                      7
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                  Homework 3


The left-hand side simplifies to (a2 + b2 )c, so

                                          (a2 + b2 )c = a.

Similarly, multiply the second equation by a and the first by b, then subtract:

                                a(ad + bc) − b(ac − bd) = 0 − b,

whose left-hand side simplifies to (a2 + b2 )d, so

                                        (a2 + b2 )d = −b.

Because a + ib ̸= 0, we have (a, b) ̸= (0, 0), hence a2 + b2 > 0 (since a2 ≥ 0 and b2 ≥ 0, and
not both are zero, we have a2 + b2 > 0) and in particular a2 + b2 ̸= 0. Therefore we may
divide by a2 + b2 to obtain
                                        a                    b
                                c=            ,        d=− 2     .
                                     a2 + b 2             a + b2
A direct substitution into (1) (or into (ac − bd) + i(ad + bc)) shows that these values indeed
yield (a + ib)(c + id) = 1 + i0. This proves the claimed formula for the multiplicative inverse.

(b) Suppose, for contradiction, that there exists an order relation < on C such that (C, +, ·, <
) is an ordered field. By Appendix (P3), we have 1 > 0 and, for any x ∈ C, x ̸= 0 ⇒ x2 > 0.
By trichotomy, exactly one of i > 0, i = 0, or i < 0 holds. We treat each case:

 • If i = 0, then i2 = 0, contradicting i2 = −1.
 • If i > 0, then i ̸= 0, so Appendix (P3) implies i2 > 0. But i2 = −1, so −1 > 0, hence
   1 < 0, contradicting 1 > 0.
 • If i < 0, then −i > 0 (using Appendix (P1) with a = i). Since −i ̸= 0, Appendix (P3)
   gives (−i)2 > 0. But (−i)2 = i2 = −1, leading again to −1 > 0 and hence 1 < 0, a
   contradiction.

All cases contradict the ordered-field consequences, so no such order relation exists.




                                                   8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                      Homework 3


Problem 4
Let A ⊆ R be a nonempty and bounded set, let c ∈ R be a number, and define

                                      B = {c · a : a ∈ A}.

(a) Prove that if c > 0, then sup B = c · sup A.
(b) Prove that if c < 0, then sup B = c · inf A.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     Main idea. We prove an equality of suprema by showing the candidate value is (i)
     an upper bound for B and (ii) the least such upper bound. The only subtlety is
     that we may multiply inequalities by a positive number without changing the direc-
     tion (ordered-field axiom (O2)), while multiplying by a negative number reverses the
     inequality (Appendix (P2)).

      • If c > 0, then a ≤ sup A implies ca ≤ c sup A, so c sup A is an upper bound for B.
        For leastness, any upper bound M of B yields ca ≤ M for all a ∈ A, hence a ≤ M/c
        after multiplying by c−1 > 0, so M/c is an upper bound for A and sup A ≤ M/c.
      • If c < 0, then inf A ≤ a implies c inf A ≥ ca, so c inf A is an upper bound for B. For
        leastness, if ca ≤ M for all a ∈ A, multiplying by c−1 < 0 reverses the inequality
        and gives a ≥ M/c, so M/c is a lower bound for A and hence M/c ≤ inf A.

Proof.
Let A ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded, let c ∈ R, and set B = {ca : a ∈ A}. We use the
definitions of sup and inf from the Appendix.

(a) Assume c > 0. We prove sup B = c sup A.
Step 1: c sup A is an upper bound for B. Let b ∈ B. Then b = ca for some a ∈ A. Because
sup A is an upper bound for A, we have a ≤ sup A. Since c > 0, multiplying by c preserves
the inequality (ordered-field axiom (O2)), so

                                          ca ≤ c sup A.

Thus b ≤ c sup A, and c sup A is an upper bound for B.
Step 2: c sup A is the least upper bound for B. Let M ∈ R be any upper bound for B. Then
for every a ∈ A, we have ca ∈ B, hence

                                            ca ≤ M.

Because c > 0, we have c ̸= 0 and c−1 > 0. Multiplying by c−1 preserves the inequality
((O2)), giving
                                        M
                            a ≤ c−1 M =    for all a ∈ A.
                                        c

                                                9
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)               Homework 3


So M/c is an upper bound for A. By definition of sup A, this implies sup A ≤ M/c. Multi-
plying by c > 0 again preserves the inequality ((O2)), so

                                       c sup A ≤ M.

Therefore c sup A is the least upper bound of B, i.e. sup B = c sup A.

(b) Assume c < 0. We prove sup B = c inf A.
Step 1: c inf A is an upper bound for B. Let b ∈ B, so b = ca for some a ∈ A. Because
inf A is a lower bound for A, we have inf A ≤ a. Since c < 0, multiplying by c reverses the
inequality (Appendix (P2)), hence

                                       c inf A ≥ ca.

Equivalently, ca ≤ c inf A, so b ≤ c inf A. Thus c inf A is an upper bound for B.
Step 2: c inf A is the least upper bound for B. Let M ∈ R be any upper bound for B. Then
for every a ∈ A,
                                           ca ≤ M.
Because c < 0, we have c ̸= 0 and c−1 < 0. Multiplying by c−1 reverses the inequality
(Appendix (P2)), so
                                          M
                            a ≥ c−1 M =        for all a ∈ A.
                                           c
Thus M/c is a lower bound for A. By definition of inf A (greatest lower bound), we get
M/c ≤ inf A. Multiplying by c < 0 reverses the inequality again (Appendix (P2)), yielding

                                       M ≥ c inf A.

So every upper bound M of B satisfies c inf A ≤ M , hence c inf A is the least upper bound
of B. Therefore sup B = c inf A.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)              Homework 3


Problem 5
Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded sets, and define
                              S = {a + b : a ∈ A and b ∈ B}.
Prove that sup S = sup A + sup B.
Discussion & Scratch Work
     Main idea. Let α := sup A and β := sup B. We show
                                        sup S = α + β
     by the standard two-step pattern for suprema:

      • Upper bound: For any s ∈ S, write s = a + b with a ∈ A, b ∈ B. Since a ≤ α
        and b ≤ β, we get s ≤ α + β. Hence α + β is an upper bound for S.
      • Leastness: If M is any upper bound for S, then for each fixed b ∈ B we have
        a + b ≤ M for all a ∈ A, hence a ≤ M − b. Thus M − b is an upper bound for A,
        so α ≤ M − b, i.e. α + b ≤ M for all b ∈ B. Therefore M − α is an upper bound
        for B, so β ≤ M − α, which gives α + β ≤ M .

     This proves α + β is the least upper bound of S.

Proof.
Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded, and set
                              S := {a + b : a ∈ A and b ∈ B}.
Let α := sup A and β := sup B.
Let s ∈ S. Then there exist a ∈ A and b ∈ B with s = a + b. Since α is an upper bound for
A and β is an upper bound for B, we have a ≤ α and b ≤ β. Adding these inequalities gives
                                       a + b ≤ α + β,
so s ≤ α + β. Hence α + β is an upper bound for S.
Let M ∈ R be any upper bound for S. Fix b ∈ B. For every a ∈ A, we have a + b ∈ S,
hence a + b ≤ M . Adding −b to both sides (by (O1)) yields a ≤ M − b for all a ∈ A, so
M − b is an upper bound for A. By definition of α = sup A, we get
                                         α ≤ M − b,
which implies α + b ≤ M . Because this holds for every b ∈ B, we have b ≤ M − α for all
b ∈ B, so M − α is an upper bound for B. By definition of β = sup B, we obtain
                                        β ≤ M − α.
Adding α to both sides gives α + β ≤ M .
Since α + β is an upper bound for S and is ≤ every upper bound M of S, it is the least
upper bound. Therefore
                           sup S = α + β = sup A + sup B.



                                              11
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                      Homework 3


Problem 6
Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded sets. Prove or disprove the following statements:

(a) If a ≤ b for all a ∈ A and for all b ∈ B, then sup A ≤ inf B.
(b) If a < b for all a ∈ A and for all b ∈ B, then sup A < inf B.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     Main ideas. Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded.

      • (a) True. The hypothesis

                                 a ≤ b for all a ∈ A and all b ∈ B

         says: every element of B is an upper bound for A. By the definition of supremum,
         sup A is below every upper bound of A, hence sup A ≤ b for all b ∈ B. That means
         sup A is a lower bound for B, so by the definition of infimum (greatest lower bound),
         sup A ≤ inf B.
      • (b) False in general. Even if a < b for all a ∈ A and b ∈ B, it can still happen
        that sup A = inf B because sup A need not belong to A. A clean counterexample is

                                      A = (0, 1),       B = {1}.

         Then a < 1 for all a ∈ (0, 1), but sup A = 1 and inf B = 1.

Proof.
Let A, B ⊆ R be nonempty and bounded.

(a) Assume that a ≤ b for all a ∈ A and all b ∈ B. We prove that sup A ≤ inf B.
Fix any b ∈ B. By the hypothesis, a ≤ b for every a ∈ A, so b is an upper bound for A
(Appendix, Definition (B1)). Let α := sup A. By the defining property of the supremum
(Appendix, Definition (S1)), we have
                                        α ≤ b.
Because b ∈ B was arbitrary, we conclude that α ≤ b for all b ∈ B, i.e. α is a lower bound
for B (Appendix, Definition (B2)).
Let β := inf B. By the defining property of the infimum (Appendix, Definition (S2)), β is
the greatest lower bound of B, so every lower bound of B is ≤ β. In particular, since α is a
lower bound of B, we obtain
                                  sup A = α ≤ β = inf B.

(b) The statement is false. Consider

                                   A := (0, 1),        B := {1}.


                                                  12
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)           Homework 3


Then A and B are nonempty and bounded subsets of R. Moreover, for every a ∈ A and
every b ∈ B we have b = 1 and 0 < a < 1, hence a < b.
However, sup A = 1 (because 1 is an upper bound for (0, 1) and any smaller number fails
to be an upper bound), and clearly inf B = 1 because B consists of the single element 1.
Therefore
                                  sup A = 1 = inf B,
so it is not true that sup A < inf B in general.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                  Homework 3


Appendix: Toolbox
Basically a summary of lectures 6—9; something like a little cheatsheet review. Anyways,
below is the good stuff.


Field axioms
(A1) (Closure for +) If a, b ∈ F , then a + b ∈ F .
(A2) (Commutativity of +) If a, b ∈ F , then a + b = b + a.
(A3) (Associativity of +) If a, b, c ∈ F , then (a + b) + c = a + (b + c).
(A4) (Additive identity) There exists 0 ∈ F such that a + 0 = 0 + a = a for all a ∈ F .
(A5) (Additive inverses) For each a ∈ F there exists −a ∈ F such that a + (−a) =
     (−a) + a = 0.

(M1) (Closure for ·) If a, b ∈ F , then a · b ∈ F .
(M2) (Commutativity of ·) If a, b ∈ F , then a · b = b · a.
(M3) (Associativity of ·) If a, b, c ∈ F , then (a · b) · c = a · (b · c).
(M4) (Multiplicative identity) There exists 1 ∈ F such that a · 1 = 1 · a = a for all
     a ∈ F.
(M5) (Multiplicative inverses) For each a ∈ F \ {0} there exists a−1 ∈ F such that
     a · a−1 = a−1 · a = 1.

 (D) (Distributivity) If a, b, c ∈ F , then (a + b) · c = a · c + b · c.


Derived properties of fields (proved from the axioms in Lecture 7)
  (1) The additive and multiplicative identities are unique.
  (2) Additive and multiplicative inverses are unique.
  (3) If a, b, c ∈ F satisfy a + b = a + c, then b = c. If a, b, c ∈ F satisfy a · b = a · c and
      a ̸= 0, then b = c.
  (4) For all a ∈ F , we have a · 0 = 0 · a = 0.
  (5) For all a, b ∈ F , we have (−a) · b = a · (−b) = −(a · b).
  (6) For all a, b ∈ F , we have (−a) · (−b) = a · b.
  (7) If a · b = 0, then a = 0 or b = 0.



                                                14
 MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)               Homework 3


 Order relations and ordered fields (Lectures 7–8)
 Order relations

 Definition (Order relation). Let A be a nonempty set. An order relation (or inequality)
 on A is a relation < on A such that:

(OR1) (Trichotomy) If a, b ∈ A, then exactly one of the following holds: a < b, or a = b,
      or b < a.
(OR2) (Transitivity) If a, b, c ∈ A satisfy a < b and b < c, then a < c.

 Notation.

   • a > b means b < a.
   • a ≤ b means (a < b) or (a = b).
   • a ≥ b means (b < a) or (a = b).

 Ordered fields

 Definition (Ordered field). Let (F, +, ·) be a field. We say that (F, +, ·, <) is an ordered
 field if it is equipped with an order relation < on F such that:

 (O1) If a, b, c ∈ F satisfy a < b, then a + c < b + c.
 (O2) If a, b, c ∈ F satisfy a < b and 0 < c, then a · c < b · c.

 Useful consequences (proved in Lecture 8). Let (F, +, ·, <) be an ordered field.

  (P1) a > 0 ⇐⇒ −a < 0.
  (P2) If a, b, c ∈ F satisfy a < b and c < 0, then a · c > b · c.
  (P3) If a ∈ F satisfies a ̸= 0, then a2 = a · a > 0. In particular, 1 > 0.
  (P4) If a, b ∈ F satisfy 0 < a < b, then 0 < b−1 < a−1 .


 Bounds, maxima/minima, and suprema/infima (Lecture 8)
 Bounds and extrema

 Definition (Bounds; max/min). Let (F, +, ·, <) be an ordered field, and let A ⊆ F be
 nonempty.

  (B1) A is bounded above if there exists M ∈ F such that a ≤ M for all a ∈ A. Any such
       M is an upper bound for A. If, in addition, M ∈ A, then M is the maximum of A.

                                                 15
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)           Homework 3


(B2) A is bounded below if there exists m ∈ F such that m ≤ a for all a ∈ A. Any such m
     is a lower bound for A. If, in addition, m ∈ A, then m is the minimum of A.
(B3) A is bounded if it is both bounded above and bounded below.

Remark (Max/min need not exist; uniqueness). Some sets do not have a maximum
(or a minimum). If a maximum exists, then it is unique: if M, M ′ ∈ A both satisfy a ≤ M
for all a ∈ A and a ≤ M ′ for all a ∈ A, then M ≤ M ′ and M ′ ≤ M , hence M = M ′ . (The
same statement holds for minima.)
Remark. Even if a set does not have a maximum, it may still have (at least one) upper
bound.

Least upper bounds and greatest lower bounds

Definition (Supremum/infimum). Let (F, +, ·, <) be an ordered field, and let A ⊆ F be
nonempty.

(S1) A number L ∈ F is the least upper bound (or supremum) of A if:

        • L is an upper bound for A, and
        • if M is an upper bound for A, then L ≤ M .

      When this is true, we write L = sup A.
(S2) A number ℓ ∈ F is the greatest lower bound (or infimum) of A if:

        • ℓ is a lower bound for A, and
        • if m is a lower bound for A, then m ≤ ℓ.

      When this is true, we write ℓ = inf A.

Lemma (Uniqueness of the supremum). If A ⊆ F has a least upper bound, then it is
unique.
Proof. Suppose L, M ∈ F are both least upper bounds for A. Then L is an upper bound
and, since M is a least upper bound, we have M ≤ L. Similarly, M is an upper bound and,
since L is a least upper bound, we have L ≤ M . Therefore L = M .


Completeness and the real numbers (Lectures 8–9)
                                  √           √
Proposition 1 (Irrationality of    2). We have 2 ∈
                                                 / Q.
                                        √
Proof. Suppose, for contradiction, that 2 ∈ Q. Then we may write
                                          √         a
                                              2=
                                                    b


                                               16
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)              Homework 3


for some integers a ∈ Z and b ∈ N with no common factors (i.e. the fraction is in lowest
terms). Squaring gives
                                   a2
                                2= 2       ⇒       2b2 = a2 .
                                   b
Thus a2 is even, hence a is even. Write a = 2k for some k ∈ Z. Substituting yields
                         2b2 = (2k)2 = 4k 2        ⇒     b2 = 2k 2 .
So b2 is even, hence b is√even. Therefore both a and b are even, contradicting that a/b was
in lowest terms. Hence 2 ∈   / Q.
Definition 1 (Least upper bound property). Let < be an order relation on a set F . We
say that F has the least upper bound property if for every nonempty subset A ⊆ F that is
bounded above, there exists a least upper bound of A that belongs to F (i.e. sup A ∈ F ).
Remark. The ordered field (Q, +, ·, <) does not have the least upper bound property. For
example,
                                A := {x ∈ Q : x2 < 2}
is nonempty
        √ and bounded above in Q, but A has no supremum in Q (its “expected” supremum
would be 2, which is not rational).
Theorem 1 (Existence and uniqueness of R as a complete ordered field). There exists an
ordered field with the least upper bound property. We denote it by
                                        (R, +, ·, <).
This ordered field is unique up to order-preserving field isomorphism, and moreover R con-
tains Q as a subfield.
Proposition 2 (Existence of infima in R). If A ⊆ R is nonempty and bounded below, then
inf A exists in R.

Proof. Assume A ⊆ R is nonempty and bounded below. Define
                                   −A := {−a : a ∈ A}.
Then −A is nonempty, and −A is bounded above (if m is a lower bound for A, then −m
is an upper bound for −A). By the least upper bound property of R, sup(−A) exists in R.
We claim that
                                 inf A = − sup(−A).
Let L := sup(−A). For any a ∈ A we have −a ∈ −A, hence −a ≤ L, i.e. −L ≤ a. So
−L is a lower bound for A. If m is any lower bound for A, then m ≤ a for all a ∈ A,
so −a ≤ −m for all a ∈ A, meaning −m is an upper bound for −A. By definition of
L = sup(−A), we have L ≤ −m, hence m ≤ −L. Thus −L is the greatest lower bound of
A, so inf A = −L = − sup(−A).
Remark (A useful identity). For any nonempty A ⊆ R that is bounded below,
                   inf A = − sup(−A),         where − A := {−a : a ∈ A}.
Conceptually: turning a lower-bound problem for A into an upper-bound problem for −A lets
us apply completeness (the LUB property).

Problem 1
Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence.

(a) Prove that if {an }n≥1 converges to a, then the sequence {|an |}n≥1 converges to |a|. (Hint:
    ||x| − |y|| ≤ |x − y| for any x, y ∈ R.)
(b) Provide an example where {|an |}n≥1 converges, but {an }n≥1 diverges.

Discussion & Scratch Work

        (a) The idea is: if an → a, then eventually an is very close to a. The absolute value
         function is “distance from 0,” so if two numbers are close, their distances from 0
         are also close. The key inequality is

                                          |x| − |y| ≤ |x − y|,

         which lets us bound |an |−|a| by |an −a| and then use the definition of convergence.
        (b) To make |an | converge while an diverges, we can alternate signs: take an =
         (−1)n . Then |an | = 1 for all n (so it converges), but an oscillates between 1 and
         −1 (so it cannot converge).

Proof.
(a) Assume an → a. Let ε > 0 be given. By convergence, there exists N ∈ N such that
for all n ≥ N we have |an − a| < ε. Using the inequality |x| − |y| ≤ |x − y| (valid for all
x, y ∈ R), with x = an and y = a, we obtain for all n ≥ N :

                                    |an | − |a| ≤ |an − a| < ε.

Hence, by the definition of convergence, |an | converges to |a|.
(b) Define an := (−1)n . Then |an | = 1 for all n, so {|an |} converges to 1. However, {an } does
not converge: if it converged to some L ∈ R, then the subsequences a2k = 1 and a2k+1 = −1
would both have to converge to L, forcing 1 = L = −1, a contradiction. Therefore {|an |}
converges while {an } diverges.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                       Homework 4


Problem 2
(Squeeze theorem). Prove that if {an }n≥1 , {bn }n≥1 , and {cn }n≥1 are sequences such that
an ≤ bn ≤ cn for all n ≥ 1 and limn→∞ an = L = limn→∞ cn , then {bn }n≥1 converges and
limn→∞ bn = L.
Discussion & Scratch Work

        Intuition: bn is “trapped” between an and cn . If both outer sequences converge to
         the same limit L, then for any small tolerance ε > 0, eventually both an and cn lie
         inside the interval (L − ε, L + ε). Since an ≤ bn ≤ cn , the middle term must also
         lie in that same interval, forcing bn → L.
        We will use the definition of convergence:

                      an → L ⇐⇒ ∀ε > 0 ∃N ∈ N s.t. ∀n ≥ N, |an − L| < ε,

         (and similarly for cn ). The only “construction” step is choosing a single index after
         which both conditions hold; we do this by taking a maximum.

Proof.
Assume that an ≤ bn ≤ cn for all n ≥ 1, and that limn→∞ an = L = limn→∞ cn . We prove
that bn → L.
Let ε > 0 be given. Since an → L, there exists N1 ∈ N such that for all n ≥ N1 ,

                          |an − L| < ε     =⇒        L − ε < an < L + ε.

Similarly, since cn → L, there exists N2 ∈ N such that for all n ≥ N2 ,

                           |cn − L| < ε    =⇒        L − ε < cn < L + ε.

Set N := max{N1 , N2 }. Then for all n ≥ N , both inequalities hold, and we have

                                 L − ε < an ≤ bn ≤ cn < L + ε.

In particular, for all n ≥ N ,
                                       L − ε < bn < L + ε,
which is equivalent to |bn − L| < ε. Since ε > 0 was arbitrary, this proves that bn → L.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                  Homework 4


Problem 3
Suppose that the sequence {an }n≥1 converges.

(a) Prove that if an ≥ m for all but finitely many n, then limn→∞ an ≥ m.
(b) Prove that if an ≤ M for all but finitely many n, then limn→∞ an ≤ M .

Discussion & Scratch Work

       The key idea is: convergence prevents the sequence from staying strictly below a
        number that it is eventually always above. If an → ℓ and (eventually) an ≥ m, then
        the limit cannot satisfy ℓ < m, because choosing ε = m−ℓ
                                                               2 would force an < ℓ+ε < m
        for all large n, contradicting an ≥ m for all large n.
       Part (b) is the symmetric argument: if (eventually) an ≤ M , then the limit cannot
        be > M .

Proof.
Let ℓ := limn→∞ an .
(a) Assume that an ≥ m for all but finitely many n. Then there exists N0 ∈ N such that

                                  an ≥ m for all n ≥ N0 .

We claim that ℓ ≥ m. Suppose for contradiction that ℓ < m. Set
                                              m−ℓ
                                       ε :=       > 0.
                                               2
Since an → ℓ, there exists N1 ∈ N such that for all n ≥ N1 ,
                                                             ℓ+m
                       |an − ℓ| < ε   =⇒      an < ℓ + ε =       < m.
                                                              2
Let N := max{N0 , N1 }. Then for all n ≥ N we have simultaneously an ≥ m and an < m, a
contradiction. Hence ℓ ≥ m.
(b) Assume that an ≤ M for all but finitely many n. Then there exists N0 ∈ N such that
an ≤ M for all n ≥ N0 . We claim that ℓ ≤ M . Suppose for contradiction that ℓ > M . Set
ε := ℓ−M
       2
         > 0. Since an → ℓ, there exists N1 ∈ N such that for all n ≥ N1 ,

                                                             ℓ+M
                       |an − ℓ| < ε   =⇒      an > ℓ − ε =       > M.
                                                              2
Let N := max{N0 , N1 }. Then for all n ≥ N we have simultaneously an ≤ M and an > M ,
a contradiction. Hence ℓ ≤ M .

Problem 4
Suppose that the sequence {an }n≥1 converges. Prove or disprove the following statements:

(a) If limn→∞ an ≥ m, then there exists N ∈ N so that an ≥ m for all n ≥ N .
(b) If limn→∞ an > m, then there exists N ∈ N so that an > m for all n ≥ N .

Discussion & Scratch Work

       Key idea. Part (a) smells suspicious because the hypothesis allows equality:
        lim an ≥ m includes the borderline case lim an = m. Convergence to m does
        not force the tail of the sequence to lie on one side of m; it only forces the tail to lie
        near m. So we should try to build a convergent sequence with limit m that stays
        strictly below m forever.
       Part (b) replaces ≥ by >, which creates a positive gap L−m. If an → L and L > m,
        then choosing ε smaller than this gap forces an to eventually lie in (L − ε, L + ε),
        which sits entirely above m. So (b) should be true, and the proof will be a direct
        ε–N argument.

Proof.
Let ℓ := limn→∞ an .
(a) Disprove. The statement is false. Fix m ∈ R and define
                                                 1
                                    an := m −           (n ≥ 1).
                                                 n
Then an → m as n → ∞, so limn→∞ an = m ≥ m holds. However, for every n ≥ 1 we have
an = m − n1 < m, so there is no N ∈ N such that an ≥ m for all n ≥ N . Hence (a) is false.
(b) Prove. Assume ℓ > m. Set
                                          ℓ−m
                                          ε :=   > 0.
                                            2
Since an → ℓ, there exists N ∈ N such that for all n ≥ N ,
                                                             ℓ−m   ℓ+m
                |an − ℓ| < ε     =⇒      an > ℓ − ε = ℓ −        =     > m.
                                                              2     2
Therefore an > m for all n ≥ N , as desired.
