Problem 5
Prove that if {an }n≥1 is a sequence
                                  √ of positive numbers that converges to a > 0, then the
          √
sequence { an }n≥1 converges to a.
Discussion & Scratch Work

        The main trick is to rewrite the difference of square-roots by “rationalizing”:
                                 √   √      |x − y|
                                | x − y| = √     √          (x, y ≥ 0).
                                             x+ y

         Here we will take x = an and y = a.
                                            √      √                  √
        Because a > 0, the denominator an + a is always at least a (since an > 0 ⇒
         √                               √       √
           an ≥ 0). So we can control | an − a| directly by controlling |an − a|.
                                                                  √
        Concretely, given ε > 0, it suffices to make |an − a| < ε a.

Proof.
Let ε > 0 be given. Since an → a, there exists N ∈ N such that for all n ≥ N ,
                                                 √
                                     |an − a| < ε a.
                                         √         √
For n ≥ N , we have an > 0 and a > 0, so an and a are defined and nonnegative. Using
                              √     √ √         √
                             ( an − a)( an + a) = an − a,

we obtain
                              √    √      |an − a| |a − a|
                             | an − a| = √      √ ≤ n√     .
                                           an + a      a
Therefore, for all n ≥ N ,
                                                       √
                              √      √     |an − a|   ε a
                             | an − a| ≤ √          < √ = ε.
                                               a        a
                                          √      √
Since ε > 0 was arbitrary, it follows that an → a.

Problem 6
                                        √                  √
Consider the following sequence: a1 =       2 and an+1 =    2 + an for all n ≥ 1.

(a) Prove that the sequence {an }n≥1 is bounded.
(b) Prove that the sequence is increasing.
(c) Conclude that the sequence converges, and find its limit.

Discussion & Scratch Work

       What we need. To show the sequence is bounded, we need to find real numbers
        m, M such that m ≤ an ≤ M for all n ≥ 1 (equivalently: bounded below and
        bounded above). To show it is increasing, we need an ≤ an+1 for all n ≥ 1.
                                             √
       Boundedness idea. Because an+1 = 2√+ an , a natural√ guess is that the sequence
        stays below 2: if an ≤
                             √ 2, then an+1 =    2 + a n ≤   4 = 2. Also an ≥ 0 implies
        an+1 ≥ 0. Since a1 = 2 ∈ (0, 2), induction should give 0 < an ≤ 2 for all n.
       Monotonicity idea. Since all terms are positive, we can compare an+1 and an by
        squaring:                      √
                     an+1 ≥ an ⇐⇒ 2 + an ≥ an ⇐⇒ 2 + an ≥ a2n .
        The last inequality is (an − 2)(an + 1) ≤ 0, which is true whenever −1 ≤ an ≤ 2.
        Our boundedness work gives 0 < an ≤ 2, so this will force an+1 ≥ an .
       Convergence + limit. Once we know {an } is increasing and bounded, we may
        invoke the Monotone Convergence Theorem (proved in lecture): the sequence
                                                                               √     con-
        verges. If an → L, then passing to the limit in the recursion gives L = 2 + L, so
        L2 − L − 2 = 0 and L ∈ {2, −1}. Since L ≥ 0, we conclude L = 2.

Proof.
(a) Boundedness. We first show that 0 < an ≤ 2 for all n ≥ 1.
                       √
Base case. We have a1 = 2, hence 0 < a1 ≤ 2.
Inductive step. Assume 0 < an ≤ 2 for some n ≥ 1. Then 2 + an ≤ 4, so
                                       √         √
                               an+1 = 2 + an ≤ 4 = 2.
                          √
Also 2 + an > 0, so an+1 = 2 + an > 0. Thus 0 < an+1 ≤ 2.
By induction, 0 < an ≤ 2 for all n ≥ 1. In particular, the sequence is bounded (below by 0
and above by 2).
(b) Increasing. Fix n ≥ 1. From part (a), an > 0, so we may square inequalities without
changing direction. We compute:
                 √
an+1 ≥ an ⇐⇒ 2 + an ≥ an ⇐⇒ 2+an ≥ a2n ⇐⇒ a2n −an −2 ≤ 0 ⇐⇒ (an −2)(an +1) ≤ 0.

But part (a) gives 0 < an ≤ 2, hence an −2 ≤ 0 and an +1 > 0, so indeed (an −2)(an +1) ≤ 0.
Therefore an+1 ≥ an for all n ≥ 1, and the sequence is increasing.

                                                7
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)            Homework 4


(c) Convergence and limit. By parts (a) and (b), {an } is bounded and increasing, so by
the Monotone Convergence Theorem it converges. Let

                                        L := lim an .
                                             n→∞
                 √
Because an+1 = 2 + an and the square-root function is continuous on (0, ∞), we may take
limits on both sides to obtain
                                   √          q                √
                 L = lim an+1 = lim 2 + an = 2 + lim an = 2 + L.
                     n→∞          n→∞                   n→∞


Squaring gives L2 = 2 + L, i.e.

                       L2 − L − 2 = 0     =⇒     (L − 2)(L + 1) = 0.

Thus L ∈ {2, −1}. Since each an > 0, we have L ≥ 0, so L ̸= −1 and therefore L = 2.

Appendix: Toolbox
Completeness / Real numbers
Proposition 1 (Archimedean property). For every x ∈ R, there exists n ∈ N such that
n > x.

Corollary 1. For every ε > 0, there exists n ∈ N such that n1 < ε.

Lemma (Integer “sandwich”). For every a ∈ R, there exists N ∈ Z such that

                                           N ≤ a < N + 1.

Proposition 2 (Q is dense in R). If x, y ∈ R satisfy x < y, then there exists q ∈ Q such
that
                                      x < q < y.

Corollary 2 (R \ Q is dense in R). If x, y ∈ R satisfy x < y, then there exists r ∈ R \ Q
such that
                                      x < r < y.


Countability
Proposition 3 (Uncountability via Cantor diagonal). Let

                                      A := {f : N → {0, 1}}.

Then A is uncountable.

Remark (Cantor’s diagonal argument (what is happening conceptually)). If we (incorrectly)
assume A is countable, we can list its elements as

                          A = {f1 , f2 , f3 , . . . },   fk : N → {0, 1}.

Think of this as an infinite table whose (k, n)-entry is fk (n) ∈ {0, 1}. We then define a new
function
                            g : N → {0, 1},      g(n) := 1 − fn (n).
This flips the diagonal entries. For each n ∈ N we have

                                             g(n) ̸= fn (n),

so g ̸= fn for every n (they differ at input n). Thus g ∈ A but g is not in our purported
enumeration, contradicting that the list contained all of A.

Corollary 3. The interval (0, 1) = {x ∈ R : 0 < x < 1} is uncountable.



                                                     9
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                 Homework 4


Sequences and limits
Definition 1 (Sequence). A sequence of real numbers is a function f : N → R. We write
an := f (n) and denote the sequence by

                                    (an )n≥1   or     {an }n≥1 .

Definition 2 (Absolute value). For x ∈ R,
                                       (
                                        x             if x ≥ 0,
                                 |x| =
                                        −x            if x < 0.

Exercise 1 (Basic properties of | · |). For all x, y ∈ R:

(a) |x| ≥ 0.
(b) |x| = 0 ⇐⇒ x = 0.
(c) |x + y| ≤ |x| + |y| (triangle inequality).
(d) |x| − |y| ≤ |x − y|.
(e) |xy| = |x||y|.

Definition 3 (Limit / convergence). We say that the sequence {an }n≥1 converges to a ∈ R
if
                       ∀ε > 0 ∃N ∈ N s.t. ∀n ≥ N, |an − a| < ε.
In this case we write limn→∞ an = a or an → a as n → ∞.

Definition 4 (Divergence). A sequence diverges if it does not converge.

Lemma (Uniqueness of limits). If {an }n≥1 converges, then its limit is unique.

Lemma (Convergent ⇒ bounded). If {an }n≥1 converges, then it is bounded.

Definition 5 (Bounded sequence). We say {an }n≥1 is bounded above / bounded below /
bounded if the set {an : n ≥ 1} is bounded above / bounded below / bounded in R.

Theorem 1 (Scalar multiple). If an → a in R and k ∈ R, then (kan ) → ka.

Theorem 2 (Limit laws). If an → a and bn → b, then:

(a) an + bn → a + b.
(b) an bn → ab.
                                            an  a
(c) If b ̸= 0 and bn ̸= 0 for all n, then      → .
                                            bn  b
Definition 6 (Monotone sequences). A sequence {an }n≥1 is


                                                 10
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — Van Vleck B119)                Homework 4


(a) increasing if an ≤ an+1 for all n ≥ 1,
(b) strictly increasing if an < an+1 for all n ≥ 1,
(c) decreasing if an ≥ an+1 for all n ≥ 1,
(d) strictly decreasing if an > an+1 for all n ≥ 1,
(e) monotone if it is increasing or decreasing.

Theorem 3 (Monotone convergence theorem). If {an }n≥1 is monotone and bounded, then
it converges.


Cauchy sequences and subsequences
Definition 7 (Cauchy sequence). A sequence {an }n≥1 is Cauchy if

                       ∀ε > 0 ∃N ∈ N s.t. ∀n, m ≥ N, |an − am | < ε.

Theorem 4 (Cauchy criterion). A sequence of real numbers is Cauchy if and only if it
converges.

Lemma (Cauchy ⇒ bounded). If {an }n≥1 is Cauchy, then it is bounded.

Definition 8 (Subsequence). Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence. We say {ahn }n≥1 is a subsequence
of {an }n≥1 if {hn }n≥1 is a strictly increasing sequence of natural numbers (i.e. hn ∈ N and
hn < hn+1 for all n).

Lemma. If {hn }n≥1 is strictly increasing in N, then hn ≥ n for all n ≥ 1.

Example 1 (Subsequences). (a) If hn = n, then {ahn } is the original sequence.
(b) If hn = 2n, then {a2n } is the even-index subsequence.
(c) If an = (−1)n , then the subsequence a2n = 1 converges (even though an diverges).

Theorem 5 (Subsequence of a convergent sequence). If an → a and {ahn } is any subse-
quence, then ahn → a.

Theorem 6 (Convergence detected by subsequences). A sequence {an } converges to a if
and only if every subsequence of {an } converges to a.

Problem 1
Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence of positive real numbers. Prove that limn→∞ an = +∞ if and
only if limn→∞ a1n = 0.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This is an “if and only if” statement, so we prove both directions using the definitions.
     Because an > 0 for all n, the reciprocal a1n is well-defined and positive. The key
     observation is that for positive numbers, taking reciprocals reverses inequalities: for
     x > 0 and M > 0,
                                                    1     1
                                    x > M ⇐⇒          <     .
                                                    x    M
     So “an eventually exceeds every M ” is the same as “ a1n eventually falls below every ε”
     (after choosing ε = 1/M or M = 1/ε).

Proof.
(⇒) Assume limn→∞ an = +∞. By definition, for every M > 0 there exists N ∈ N such
that an > M for all n ≥ N . Let ε > 0 be given and choose M = 1/ε (note M > 0). Then
there exists N such that for all n ≥ N ,
                                                 1
                                             an > .
                                                 ε
Since an > 0, taking reciprocals yields
                                   1
                                      <ε        for all n ≥ N.
                                   an

Thus for all n ≥ N we have      1
                               an
                                  −0   = a1n < ε, which shows limn→∞ a1n = 0.
(⇐) Assume limn→∞ a1n = 0. By definition, for every ε > 0 there exists N ∈ N such that for
all n ≥ N ,
                                       1
                                         − 0 < ε.
                                      an
Because a1n > 0, this is the same as a1n < ε. Now let M > 0 be given and choose ε = 1/M
(note ε > 0). Then there exists N such that for all n ≥ N ,
                                            1    1
                                               <   .
                                            an   M
Since an > 0, taking reciprocals reverses the inequality and gives

                                  an > M        for all n ≥ N.

This is exactly the definition of limn→∞ an = +∞.

Problem 2
Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence. Prove that if {akn }n≥1 is a subsequence of {an }n≥1 that converges
to L ∈ R, then
                                lim inf an ≤ L ≤ lim sup an .
                                  n→∞                   n→∞

(Hint: As {an }n≥1 is not necessarily bounded, we have to consider the cases lim inf n→∞ an =
−∞ and lim supn→∞ an = +∞. However, the proof is much easier in these cases.)
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The core idea is: compare each subsequence term akn to the tail bounds of the original
     sequence. Fix a tail starting at N . Then

                    uN := inf{am : m ≥ N }      and    vN := sup{am : m ≥ N }

     satisfy uN ≤ am ≤ vN for every m ≥ N .
     Now use the fact that kn → ∞ (because {kn } is strictly increasing), so for any fixed
     N , eventually kn ≥ N . Hence for all large n,

                                        uN ≤ akn ≤ vN .

     Taking n → ∞ gives uN ≤ L ≤ vN for each N . Finally, let N → ∞: by definition,

                        lim inf an = lim uN ,         lim sup an = lim vN ,
                         n→∞        N →∞               n→∞        N →∞

     and passing to the limit preserves the inequalities. (If lim inf = −∞ or lim sup = +∞,
     the corresponding inequality is automatic.)

Proof.
Let {akn }n≥1 be a subsequence of {an }n≥1 and suppose

                                       lim akn = L ∈ R.
                                      n→∞


Case 1: lim inf n→∞ an = −∞. Then lim inf n→∞ an ≤ L holds automatically, since −∞ ≤ L.
Case 2: lim supn→∞ an = +∞. Then L ≤ lim supn→∞ an holds automatically, since L ≤ +∞.
So assume from now on that {an } is bounded (equivalently, both lim inf an and lim sup an
are finite real numbers). For each N ∈ N define the tail infimum and tail supremum

                    uN = inf{am : m ≥ N },            vN = sup{am : m ≥ N }.

By definition of infimum and supremum, for every m ≥ N we have

                                        uN ≤ am ≤ vN .

Fix N ∈ N. Because {kn } is strictly increasing, we have kn ≥ n for all n, and in particular
there exists n0 such that kn ≥ N for all n ≥ n0 . Therefore, for all n ≥ n0 ,

                                        uN ≤ akn ≤ vN .

                                                 3
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                  Homework 5


Now take the limit as n → ∞. Since uN and vN are constants (in n) and akn → L, we obtain

                            uN ≤ L ≤ vN       for every N ∈ N.

Finally, let N → ∞. By definition,

                     lim inf an = lim uN ,       lim sup an = lim vN .
                      n→∞        N →∞             n→∞          N →∞


Taking limits in the inequality uN ≤ L ≤ vN gives

                               lim inf an ≤ L ≤ lim sup an ,
                                n→∞                 n→∞

as desired.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                                     Homework 5


Problem 3
Let {an }n≥1 and {bn }n≥1 be two bounded sequences. Prove that

                              lim sup(an + bn ) ≤ lim sup an + lim sup bn .
                               n→∞                         n→∞                          n→∞


Discussion & Scratch Work

     We work directly from the definition of lim sup via tail suprema. We know that they
     exist for an and bn because the sequences are bounded. We also know they exist for
     an + bn because the sum of two bounded sequences is bounded (which I think we
     prove by considering the definition of bounded; if an is bounded, then there exists
     M1 > 0 such that |an | ≤ M for all n, and similarly for bn which is bounded by M2 , so
     |an + bn | ≤ |an | + |bn | ≤ by the triangle inequality so so an + bn is bounded by M1 + M2
     which we could call K ∈ R s.t. ∀n ∈ N, |an + bn | ≤ K and thus an + bn is bounded).
     Anyway, check out these definitions:
     Since {an } and {bn } are bounded, for each N ∈ N we define

                        (a)                                          (b)
                      vN = sup{an : n ≥ N },                      vN = sup{bn : n ≥ N },

     and

                                      (a+b)
                                     vN       = sup{an + bn : n ≥ N }.

     For every n ≥ N , we have
                                                   (a)                            (b)
                                       an ≤ vN            and        bn ≤ vN .

     Adding these inequalities gives
                                                               (a)          (b)
                                          an + bn ≤ vN + vN .

     Since this holds for all n ≥ N , the supremum satisfies
                                              (a+b)         (a)            (b)
                                              vN         ≤ vN + vN .

     Now take limits as N → ∞ and use the definition of lim sup.
     (Please don’t make us prove this again this was so saucey)

Proof.
Since {an } and {bn } are bounded sequences, for each N ∈ N the sets {an : n ≥ N } and
{bn : n ≥ N } are nonempty and bounded. Define
                      (a)                                            (b)
                    vN = sup{an : n ≥ N },                        vN = sup{bn : n ≥ N },


                                                           5
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                                            Homework 5


and
                                   (a+b)
                               vN          = sup{an + bn : n ≥ N }.

For every n ≥ N , we have
                                                (a)                         (b)
                                    an ≤ vN             and bn ≤ vN .
Therefore
                                                             (a)      (b)
                                       an + bn ≤ vN + vN .
Since this inequality holds for all n ≥ N , taking the supremum over n ≥ N gives
                                            (a+b)         (a)        (b)
                                           vN         ≤ vN + vN .

Now take limits as N → ∞. By definition,
                                                                            (a+b)
                                  lim sup(an + bn ) = lim vN                        ,
                                   n→∞                             N →∞

                     lim sup an = lim vN ,                      lim sup bn = lim vN .
                       n→∞            N →∞                         n→∞                  N →∞

                                                (a+b)        (a)      (b)
Passing to the limit in the inequality vN               ≤ vN + vN yields

                         lim sup(an + bn ) ≤ lim sup an + lim sup bn .

Problem 4
Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence. Prove that lim supn→∞ |an | = 0 if and only if limn→∞ an = 0.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This is an “if and only if” statement, so I will prove both directions.
     A helpful way for me to think about lim sup is via tail suprema:
                                                                 
                            lim sup xn = lim sup{xn : n ≥ N }
                               n→∞          N →∞

     whenever the sequence {xn } is bounded above (and if it is not bounded above, then
     lim sup xn = +∞).
     Here I take xn = |an | ≥ 0. Intuitively:

        If an → 0, then eventually |an | is small; therefore every tail supremum sup{|an | :
         n ≥ N } must also be small, forcing lim sup |an | = 0.
        Conversely, if the tail suprema of |an | tend to 0, then (in particular) every term
         in a sufficiently far tail must be smaller than ε, which is exactly the definition of
         an → 0.

Proof.
(⇒) Assume limn→∞ an = 0. Let ε > 0 be given. By definition of convergence, there exists
N0 ∈ N such that
                       |an − 0| = |an | < ε   for all n ≥ N0 .
Now fix any N ≥ N0 . Then for every n ≥ N I have |an | < ε, and hence

                                      sup{|an | : n ≥ N } ≤ ε.

Define vN := sup{|an | : n ≥ N }. The inequality above shows that for all N ≥ N0 , vN ≤ ε.
Taking N → ∞ yields
                                 lim sup |an | = lim vN ≤ ε.
                                     n→∞               N →∞

Because ε > 0 was arbitrary and lim supn→∞ |an | ≥ 0 (since |an | ≥ 0), I conclude lim supn→∞ |an | =
0.
(⇐) Assume lim supn→∞ |an | = 0. Set again

                                     vN := sup{|an | : n ≥ N }.

By definition of lim sup (and since |an | ≥ 0), I have limN →∞ vN = 0. Let ε > 0 be given.
Then there exists N0 ∈ N such that
                                           vN0 < ε.
But for every n ≥ N0 I have |an | ≤ vN0 (because vN0 is the supremum of the tail {|an | : n ≥
N0 }). Therefore, for all n ≥ N0 ,
                                      |an | ≤ vN0 < ε.
This is exactly the definition of an → 0. Hence limn→∞ an = 0.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                      Homework 5


Appendix: Toolbox
Sequences and Cauchy
Definition 1. We say that {an }n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence if: for all ε > 0 there exists N ∈ N
such that |an − am | < ε for all m, n ≥ N .
Theorem 1 (Cauchy criterion). Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence. The sequence {an }n≥1 is Cauchy
if and only if it converges.
Proposition 1. If {an }n≥1 converges, then it is Cauchy.
Lemma. If {an }n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence, then it is bounded.
Proposition 2. If {an }n≥1 is a Cauchy sequence, then it converges.


Subsequences
Definition 2. Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence. We say {akn }n≥1 is a subsequence of {an }n≥1 if
{kn }n≥1 is a strictly increasing sequence of natural numbers: kn ∈ N and kn < kn+1 for all
n ∈ N.
Remark. Any such {kn }n≥1 must satisfy kn ≥ n for all n ≥ 1. This is easy to prove by
induction. (Exercise.)
Theorem 2. Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence and a ∈ R. The sequence {an }n≥1 converges to a if
and only if every subsequence {akn }n≥1 converges to a.


Monotone Subsequences
Proposition 3. For any sequence, there is a subsequence that is monotone.
Theorem 3 (Bolzano–Weierstrass). For any bounded sequence, there exists a subsequence
that converges.


Divergence to ±∞
Definition 3. Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence. We say:

(1) {an }n≥1 diverges to +∞ if for all M > 0 there exists N ∈ N such that an > M for all
    n ≥ N . When this is true, we write limn→∞ an = +∞.
(2) {an }n≥1 diverges to −∞ if for all m < 0 there exists N ∈ N such that an < m for all
    n ≥ N . When this is true, we write limn→∞ an = −∞.
Proposition 4. If {an }n≥1 is unbounded and increasing (decreasing), then {an }n≥1 diverges
to +∞ (−∞, respectively).

                                             8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                          Homework 5


Remark. If a sequence is increasing, there are only two possibilities:

(1) bounded ⇒ converges;
(2) unbounded ⇒ diverges to +∞.
Remark. “lim an ” only sometimes makes sense:

(1) converges ⇒ limn→∞ an = a ∈ R;
(2) diverges: diverges to ±∞ ⇒ limn→∞ an = ±∞, else limn→∞ an does not exist.


Limit Superior and Limit Inferior
Definition 4. Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence (convergent or not). Case 1: {an }n≥1 is bounded.
Fix any N ∈ N. Then the set {an : n ≥ N } is nonempty and bounded, so
                     uN = inf{an : n ≥ N },         vN = sup{an : n ≥ N }
both exist. We automatically have uN ≤ vN for all N .
Proposition 5. With uN and vN as above, {uN }N ≥1 is increasing and {vN }N ≥1 is decreasing.
In particular,
                 u1 ≤ u2 ≤ · · · ≤ uN ≤ vN ≤ · · · ≤ v2 ≤ v1      for all N ≥ 1.
Proposition 6. The sequences {uN }N ≥1 and {vN }N ≥1 are both monotone and bounded, and
therefore converge. Set
                           u = lim uN         and         v = lim vN .
                               N →∞                           N →∞

Then u ≤ v. Moreover, if limn→∞ an = a exists, then u ≤ a ≤ v.
Definition 5. Let {an }n≥1 be a bounded sequence. We define
                                                             
                    lim sup an = lim vN = lim sup{an : n ≥ N }
                       n→∞        N →∞         N →∞

and                                                                      
                     lim inf an = lim uN = lim            inf{an : n ≥ N } .
                      n→∞         N →∞        N →∞

Corollary 1. If {an }n≥1 is bounded, then
                                   lim inf an ≤ lim sup an .
                                    n→∞             n→∞

Definition 6. If {an }n≥1 is not bounded above, then we define
                                      lim sup an = +∞.
                                       n→∞

If {an }n≥1 is not bounded below, then we define
                                      lim inf an = −∞.
                                       n→∞


                                               9
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                  Homework 5


Remark. So lim sup and lim inf make sense for any sequence, but their values are in R ∪
{±∞}.

Corollary 2. For any sequence {an }n≥1 , we have

                                 lim inf an ≤ lim sup an .
                                  n→∞            n→∞

Theorem 4. Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence.

(1) If limn→∞ an exists in R ∪ {±∞}, then

                              lim inf an = lim an = lim sup an .
                               n→∞          n→∞         n→∞


(2) If lim inf n→∞ an = lim supn→∞ an in R ∪ {±∞}, then limn→∞ an exists and equals this
    value.

Remark. Here, “limn→∞ an exists in R∪{±∞}” just means “{an }n≥1 converges, or diverges
to +∞, or diverges to −∞.”

      that if ∞

            a b
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The goal is to use the Cauchy criterion for series.
     Since ∞
          P
            n=1 an converges absolutely, the series

                                                     ∞

                                                           |an |
                                                     n=1

     converges. Therefore, by the Cauchy criterion, for every ε > 0 there exists N ∈ N such

Cesàro–Stolz
Theorem (Cesàro–Stolz). Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence of nonzero real numbers. Then

                        an+1                                                 an+1
              lim inf        ≤ lim inf |an |1/n ≤ lim sup |an |1/n ≤ lim sup      .
                n→∞      an      n→∞                n→∞                n→∞    an

Lemma. If r > 0, then limn→∞ r1/n = 1.


Series: Definitions and Basic Facts
                                                                                       P∞
Definition (Series). Let {an }n≥1 be a sequence of real numbers. The series             n=1 an
converges/diverges if the sequence of partial sums sn = a1 + · · · + an converges/diverges.
                                                         P∞
Definition
      P∞ (Absolute Convergence). The series                n=1 an converges absolutely if the
series n=1 |an | converges.
Remark. As |an | ≥ 0, the sequence sn = |a1 | + · · · + |an | is increasing, so either ∞
                                                                                      P
                                                                                       n=1 |an |
converges or diverges to +∞.
Corollary. If ∞
              P
                  n=1 an converges, then limn→∞ an = 0.
Remark (Geometric Series). The series ∞              n
                                             P
                                                n=0 r converges if |r| < 1 and

                                               ∞
                                               X             1
                                                     rn =       .
                                               n=0
                                                            1−r
                   P∞       n
If |r| ≥ 1, then    n=0 r       diverges.


Cauchy Criterion for Series
                                                        P∞
Theorem (Cauchy criterion). The series                      n=1 an converges if and only if for all ε > 0
there exists N ∈ N such that
                                     n
                                     X
                                            ak < ε for all n ≥ m ≥ N.
                                     k=m



Absolute Convergence
                P∞
Corollary. If      n=1 an converges absolutely, then it converges.



Comparison Test
                                               P∞
Theorem (Comparison test). Let                   n=1 an be a series with an ≥ 0 for all n ≥ 1.


                                                       14
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                    Homework 6

         P∞                                                   P∞
(1) If n=1 a n converges and |b n | ≤ a n for all n ≥ 1, then  n=1 bn converges.
      P∞                                                   P∞
(2) If n=1 an diverges and bn ≥ an for all n ≥ 1, then n=1 bn diverges.

                     P∞ 1

Problem 1
For each of the following series, determine if it converges and prove your answer.

(a)
                                                 ∞
                                                 X     1
                                                 n=2
                                                     log n

(b)
                                                ∞
                                                X n−1
                                                n=1
                                                         n2

(c)
                                                  ∞
                                                  X n2
                                                  n=1
                                                         n!

Discussion & Scratch Work

      For each part, I first identify the general type of positive-term series and then choose
      the test that best matches its dominant behavior.
      (a) The terms
                                                   1
                                                 log n
      go to zero extremely slowly, and they are larger than the harmonic terms 1/n for n ≥ 2.
      This strongly suggests divergence. A direct comparison with the harmonic series is the
      cleanest method here, because it proves divergence immediately. The dyadic criterion
      could also be used, but comparison is shorter and more transparent.
      (b) The terms
                                                n−1
                                                 n2
      behave like 1/n for large n, so I again expect divergence. I could rewrite the term as
                                               1   1
                                                 − 2,
                                               n n
      which suggests that the harmonic part should dominate, but a direct lower comparison
      with 1/(2n) is cleaner. This makes the comparison test the most straightforward choice.
      (c) The factorial in the denominator grows much faster than the polynomial n2 in the
      numerator, so I expect the terms to shrink very rapidly and the series to converge.
      Because the expression involves a factorial, the ratio test is the natural tool: successive
      terms simplify nicely, whereas a direct comparison would be less immediate. The ratio
      tends to 0, which confirms convergence.




                                                  2
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                      Homework 7

Problem 2
For each of the following series, determine if it converges and prove your answer.

(a)
                                                ∞

                                              ∞

Discussion & Scratch Work

      For each part, I first look at the dominant growth in the numerator and denominator,
      and then choose the test that makes that growth comparison easiest to formalize.
      (a) The terms
                                                 n4
                                                 4n
      have polynomial growth in the numerator and exponential growth in the denominator.
      Exponential growth should dominate, so I expect convergence. Since the expression is
      of the form “polynomial over exponential,” the ratio test is natural: passing from an
      to an+1 simplifies cleanly and the ratio should tend to 14 < 1. A comparison test could
      also work, but the ratio test is more direct.
      (b) The terms
                                                n!
                                              n4 + 3
      have factorial growth in the numerator and only polynomial growth in the denominator.
      Since factorial growth is much faster, I expect the terms not even to go to 0, which
      would force divergence immediately. The ratio test is the cleanest way to confirm this
      expectation, because it captures the factorial growth very efficiently. A direct term
      test would also be possible after enough asymptotic work, but the ratio test is more
      systematic.
      (c) The terms
                                                2n
                                                n!
      have exponential growth in the numerator but factorial growth in the denominator.
      Since factorial growth dominates exponential growth, I expect convergence. Again, the
      ratio test is the most natural choice because the factorial causes strong cancellation
      between successive terms, and the ratio should go to 0 < 1.

Problem 3
For each of the following series, determine if it converges and prove your answer.

(a)
                                                ∞
                                                X 1
                                                n=1
                                                      nn

(b)
                                                ∞
                                                X n!
                                                n=1
                                                      nn

(c)
                                             ∞
                                             X (−1)n n!
                                              n=1
                                                      2n

Discussion & Scratch Work

      For each part, I first compare the general size of the terms to the type of test that is
      best adapted to them.
      (a) The terms
                                                 1
                                                nn
      decay extremely fast, much faster than an ordinary geometric or p-series. Since the
      exponent itself depends on n, the root test is the most natural choice: taking the nth
      root removes the varying exponent immediately. A comparison test could be made to
      work, but the root test is much cleaner.
      (b) The terms
                                               n!
                                               nn
      also involve competing growth rates, now factorial versus power. Because factorial
      expressions simplify very well under the ratio test, that is the most efficient method
      here. The ratio should approach e−1 -type behavior intuitively, and in fact it will tend
      to a number strictly less than 1, which proves convergence. The root test is possible
      too, but the ratio test is more direct.
      (c) The terms
                                              (−1)n n!
                                                 2n
      have alternating sign, but the factorial in the numerator grows much faster than the
      exponential in the denominator. So before thinking about alternating-series ideas, I
      should check whether the terms even go to 0. The ratio test is the cleanest way to
      capture this growth. Since the absolute ratio will tend to something larger than 1, the
      terms cannot go to 0, so the series must diverge.



                                                 8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                                             Homework 7

                                  n→∞                   n→∞ n

                                                            nn
converges absolutely. In particular, it converges.
(b) Consider the series
                                                ∞

                                                                 .

                                                         nn

Problem 4
Consider the series
                        ∞
                        X         n        1 1  1  1  1   1

                        n=1

(a) Show that the ratio test is inconclusive.
(b) Prove that the series converges using the root test.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem tells me which tests to use, so the main task is to apply them carefully
     and explicitly.
     (a) To show that the ratio test is inconclusive, I should not just say that the limit
     does not exist. I need to compute the ratio of consecutive terms and show that its tail
     behavior crosses both sides of 1. Because the exponent (−1)n depends on parity, the
     ratio will behave differently for even and odd n. So the cleanest way is to compute
     an+1 /an and then split into the two parity cases.
     (b) Once the ratio test fails, the root test is more natural because the nth root interacts
     well with the factor 2−n . After taking the nth root, the only remaining subtlety is the
                 n
     factor 2(−1) /n , which tends to 1 since (−1)n /n → 0. This should give a limit strictly
     less than 1, so the series converges absolutely.

Problem 6
Prove or disprove the following statements:
         P∞                    P∞
(a) If n=1 a n converges, then  n=1 a2n converges.

(b) If n=1 an converges absolutely, then ∞
      P∞                                 P
                                           n=1 a2n converges absolutely.


Discussion & Scratch Work

       The key point in both parts is that the series
                                                ∞
                                                X
                                                      a2n
                                                n=1

       keeps only the even-indexed terms of the original series. So I should ask whether
       passing to a subsequence of terms preserves convergence.
       (a) I do not expect ordinary convergence to be preserved, because a convergent series
       may rely on cancellation between its odd and even terms. If I keep only the even
       terms, I may destroy that cancellation completely. The alternating harmonic series
       is the standard example to test this suspicion: the full series converges, but the even
       terms alone look like a constant multiple of the harmonic series. So this statement
       should be false, and a counterexample is the right method.
       (b) Absolute convergence is much stronger. If
                                                ∞
                                                X
                                                      |an |
                                                n=1

       converges, then throwing away terms should not break convergence, because the re-
       maining terms form a positive subseries of a convergent positive series. So I expect
       this statement to be true. The cleanest proof is a direct comparison:
                                                                   X
                            0 ≤ |a2n | ≤ (the full positive series   |an |),

       which shows that the even-indexed absolute-value series also converges.

Metric Spaces
Definition 3 (Metric). Let X be a nonempty set. A metric on X is a function

                                          d:X ×X →R

such that:

(1) d(x, y) ≥ 0 for all x, y ∈ X.
(2) d(x, y) = 0 if and only if x = y.
(3) d(x, y) = d(y, x) for all x, y ∈ X.
(4) (Triangle inequality) d(x, z) ≤ d(x, y) + d(y, z) for all x, y, z ∈ X.

Definition 4 (Metric space). Let X be a nonempty set and let d be a metric on X. Together,
we call (X, d) a metric space.

Problem 1
Consider the metric space (R, d) with the usual metric d(x, y) = |x − y|. For each of the
following sets A ⊆ R, find its interior and prove your answer.

(a) A = [0, 1)
(b) A = Z
(c) A = {x ∈ Q : 0 < x < 1}

Discussion & Scratch Work

     For part (a), I want to identify exactly which points of A = [0, 1) admit a small open
     ball that stays entirely inside A.
     Because the metric is the usual one on R,

                         Br (x) = {y ∈ R : |x − y| < r} = (x − r, x + r).

     So the problem becomes: for which points x ∈ [0, 1) does there exist r > 0 such that

                                      (x − r, x + r) ⊆ [0, 1)?

     The natural guess is that
                                           A◦ = (0, 1).
     Indeed, if x ∈ (0, 1), then x has positive distance from both endpoints 0 and 1, so I
     should be able to choose a small radius that stays inside the interval. On the other
     hand, the endpoint 0 should fail to be interior, because every open ball around 0 extends
     to the left of 0, hence leaves A.
     So the proof has two parts:

     (1) show that every x ∈ (0, 1) is an interior point of A by constructing an explicit
         radius r > 0;
     (2) show that 0 is not an interior point of A.

     Since A = [0, 1) contains only the points in (0, 1) together with 0, these two steps will
     identify the interior completely.
     For part (b), I again translate the definition of interior point into balls for the usual
     metric. Since
                                     Br (n) = (n − r, n + r)
     for any integer n ∈ Z, the question is whether there can be some radius r > 0 for which
     this entire interval stays inside Z. The natural guess is that this is impossible, because
     every open interval in R contains non-integer real numbers. So I expect that no integer
     is an interior point, and hence
                                              Z◦ = ∅.



                                                 2
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                             Homework 8


      To prove this, I take an arbitrary integer n and an arbitrary radius r > 0, and then I
      exhibit a specific point in Br (n) that is not an integer. This shows that no open ball
      centered at an integer can stay inside Z, so no point of Z is interior.
      For part (c), I consider
                                     A = {x ∈ Q : 0 < x < 1}.
      Although the rationals are dense in R, that does not help with interior points. In fact,
      every open interval in R contains irrational numbers as well. This suggests that no
      open ball centered at a rational point can stay entirely inside A, since A contains only
      rationals.
      So I suspect that
                                               A◦ = ∅.
      To prove this, I take an arbitrary point x ∈ A and an arbitrary radius r > 0, and show
      that Br (x) is not contained in A. Using the density of the irrationals, I can always
      find an irrational number inside (x − r, x + r), which will not belong to A. This shows
      that no point of A is an interior point.

Proof.
(a) Let A = [0, 1). I claim that
                                             A◦ = (0, 1).

First, let x ∈ (0, 1). Define
                                       1
                                         min{x, 1 − x}.
                                      r :=
                                       2
Since x > 0 and 1 − x > 0, we have r > 0. Also,
                                      x                        1−x
                                 r≤           and        r≤        ,
                                      2                         2
so
                                 x−r >0          and         x + r < 1.
Therefore,
                          Br (x) = (x − r, x + r) ⊆ (0, 1) ⊆ [0, 1) = A.
Hence x is an interior point of A. Since x ∈ (0, 1) was arbitrary, it follows that

                                             (0, 1) ⊆ A◦ .

Now we show that 0 is not an interior point of A. Let r > 0. Then

                                          Br (0) = (−r, r).

But
                              r                                  r
                             − ∈ (−r, r)         and         −     ∈
                                                                   / [0, 1).
                              2                                  2
So
                                             Br (0) ⊈ A.

                                                  3
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                        Homework 8


Since this happens for every r > 0, the point 0 is not an interior point of A.
Because the only points of A = [0, 1) are the points of (0, 1) together with 0, we conclude
that the interior of A is exactly
                                       A◦ = (0, 1).
(b) Let A = Z. I claim that
                                          A◦ = ∅.

Let n ∈ Z. We show that n is not an interior point of A. Let r > 0. Then

                                   Br (n) = (n − r, n + r).

Since (n − r, n + r) is an open interval in R, it contains points that are not integers. In
particular, we can choose some y ∈ (n − r, n + r) with y ∈
                                                         / Z. Then

                                  y ∈ Br (n) but y ∈
                                                   / Z,

so
                                         Br (n) ⊈ Z.
Since this happens for every r > 0, the point n is not an interior point of Z.
Because n ∈ Z was arbitrary, no point of Z is an interior point. Therefore

                                          Z◦ = ∅.

(c) Let
                                 A = {x ∈ Q : 0 < x < 1}.
I claim that
                                          A◦ = ∅.

Let x ∈ A. We show that x is not an interior point of A. Let r > 0. Then

                                   Br (x) = (x − r, x + r).

Since R \ Q is dense in R, there exists an irrational number y such that

                                     x − r < y < x + r.

Hence
                                         y ∈ Br (x),
but y ∈
      / A, since A ⊆ Q. Therefore
                                         Br (x) ⊈ A.
Since this happens for every r > 0, the point x is not an interior point of A.
Because x ∈ A was arbitrary, no point of A is an interior point. Thus

                                          A◦ = ∅.

Problem 2
Recall that d1 and d∞ are both metrics on R2 .

(a) Prove that d∞ (x, y) ≤ d1 (x, y) ≤ 2d∞ (x, y) for all x, y ∈ R2 .
(b) Prove that for any x ∈ R2 and r > 0 we have
          {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < 2r } ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r}.
    I recommend that you draw a picture of these three sets.
(c) Prove that a set A ⊆ R2 is open in the metric space (R2 , d1 ) if and only if it is open in
    the metric space (R2 , d∞ ).

Discussion & Scratch Work
      (a) This part is asking me to compare the two metrics on R2 pointwise. So for arbitrary
      points x = (x1 , x2 ) and y = (y1 , y2 ), I should write out both definitions:
            d∞ (x, y) = max{|x1 − y1 |, |x2 − y2 |},   d1 (x, y) = |x1 − y1 | + |x2 − y2 |.
      The first inequality should follow because the maximum of two nonnegative numbers
      is at most their sum, and the second should follow because each term is at most the
      maximum. So this is mostly an algebraic comparison of two formulas.
      (b) This part translates the inequalities from part (a) into containments of open balls.
      To prove one set is contained in another, I should take an arbitrary point in the first
      set and use the distance inequality from part (a) to show it belongs to the second set.
      So each inclusion should be a short direct argument using the definitions of the balls
      and the inequalities already proved. Check out the diagram below (it’s pretty and
      awesome):




Figure 1: The three nested sets centered at x with parameter r = 2: the inner blue square
is {d∞ (x, y) < r/2}, the amber diamond is {d1 (x, y) < r}, and the outer teal square is
{d∞ (x, y) < r}.

                                                  5
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                                     Homework 8


        (c) This part is asking me to show that the notions of open set coming from d1 and
        d∞ are the same. The key idea is that part (b) tells me that around any point, a
        sufficiently small d∞ -ball sits inside a given d1 -ball, and conversely a d1 -ball sits inside
        a corresponding d∞ -ball. So to prove openness in one metric implies openness in the
        other, I take a point of the set, start with the open ball guaranteed by one metric, and
        then use part (b) to find a smaller ball for the other metric that is still contained in
        the set.

Proof.
(a) Let x = (x1 , x2 ) and y = (y1 , y2 ) be points in R2 . Then

                                  d∞ (x, y) = max{|x1 − y1 |, |x2 − y2 |}

and
                                     d1 (x, y) = |x1 − y1 | + |x2 − y2 |.
Because |x1 − y1 | and |x2 − y2 | are nonnegative, we have

                           max{|x1 − y1 |, |x2 − y2 |} ≤ |x1 − y1 | + |x2 − y2 |,

so
                                           d∞ (x, y) ≤ d1 (x, y).
Also,
                           |x1 − y1 | ≤ max{|x1 − y1 |, |x2 − y2 |} = d∞ (x, y)
and similarly
                                           |x2 − y2 | ≤ d∞ (x, y).
Adding these inequalities gives

                             d1 (x, y) = |x1 − y1 | + |x2 − y2 | ≤ 2d∞ (x, y).

Therefore,
                                    d∞ (x, y) ≤ d1 (x, y) ≤ 2d∞ (x, y)
for all x, y ∈ R2 .
(b) Let x ∈ R2 and let r > 0.
We first prove that

                        {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r/2} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r}.

Let y ∈ R2 satisfy d∞ (x, y) < r/2. By part (a),
                                                                    r
                                    d1 (x, y) ≤ 2d∞ (x, y) < 2 ·      = r.
                                                                    2
Hence y ∈ {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r}. This proves the first inclusion.
Now we prove that

                          {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r}.

                                                      6
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                           Homework 8


Let y ∈ R2 satisfy d1 (x, y) < r. By part (a),

                                    d∞ (x, y) ≤ d1 (x, y) < r.

Hence y ∈ {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r}. This proves the second inclusion.
Therefore,

       {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r/2} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r}.

(c) We prove that a set A ⊆ R2 is open in (R2 , d1 ) if and only if it is open in (R2 , d∞ ).
Suppose first that A is open in (R2 , d1 ). Let x ∈ A. Since A is open with respect to d1 , there
exists r > 0 such that
                                 {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r} ⊆ A.
By part (b),
                     {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r/2} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r}.
Therefore,
                                {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r/2} ⊆ A.
So x is an interior point of A with respect to d∞ . Since x ∈ A was arbitrary, A is open in
(R2 , d∞ ).
Conversely, suppose that A is open in (R2 , d∞ ). Let x ∈ A. Since A is open with respect to
d∞ , there exists r > 0 such that

                                 {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r} ⊆ A.

By part (b),
                      {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r} ⊆ {y ∈ R2 : d∞ (x, y) < r}.
Therefore,
                                  {y ∈ R2 : d1 (x, y) < r} ⊆ A.
So x is an interior point of A with respect to d1 . Since x ∈ A was arbitrary, A is open in
(R2 , d1 ).
Hence a set A ⊆ R2 is open in (R2 , d1 ) if and only if it is open in (R2 , d∞ ).

Problem 3
Let X be a nonempty set, and recall that the discrete metric d is a metric on X. Prove that
every set A ⊆ X is open in the metric space (X, d).
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem is asking me to use the definition of openness in a very specific metric:
     the discrete metric. In the discrete metric, distinct points are always distance 1 apart,
     so the key observation is that a sufficiently small open ball around any point should
     contain only that point.
     So to prove that an arbitrary set A ⊆ X is open, I take an arbitrary point a ∈ A and
     try to find a radius r > 0 such that

                                           Br (a) ⊆ A.

     The natural choice is any radius with 0 < r ≤ 1, since then the ball around a should be
     just {a}. Once I show that, it follows immediately that every point of A is an interior
     point, and hence A is open.

Proof.
Let A ⊆ X. We will show that A is open in (X, d).
Let a ∈ A. Since d is the discrete metric, we have
                                             (
                                              0 if x = a,
                                   d(a, x) =
                                              1 if x ̸= a.

Consider the open ball of radius 21 centered at a:

                              B1/2 (a) = {x ∈ X : d(a, x) < 1/2}.

If x = a, then d(a, x) = 0 < 1/2, so a ∈ B1/2 (a). On the other hand, if x ̸= a, then
d(a, x) = 1 ̸< 1/2, so x ∈
                         / B1/2 (a).
Therefore,
                                         B1/2 (a) = {a}.
Since a ∈ A, we have {a} ⊆ A, and hence

                                          B1/2 (a) ⊆ A.

So a is an interior point of A.
Because a ∈ A was arbitrary, every point of A is an interior point. Therefore

                                             A◦ = A,

so A is open in (X, d).

Problem 5
Let (X, d) be a metric space, A ⊆ X, and x ∈ X. Prove that x is in A◦ if and only if there
exists an open set U so that x ∈ U ⊆ A.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem is asking me to connect two ways of saying that a point lies in the interior
     of a set. By definition, x ∈ A◦ means that there is an open ball around x contained in
     A. On the other hand, the statement involving an open set U says there is some open
     neighborhood of x contained in A.
     So the proof should go in both directions. If x ∈ A◦ , then I can simply take the open
     ball from the definition and call it U . Conversely, if I already have an open set U with
     x ∈ U ⊆ A, then because U is open and contains x, there must be an open ball around
     x contained in U , hence contained in A. That is exactly the definition of x ∈ A◦ .

Proof.
We prove that

             x ∈ A◦    ⇐⇒      there exists an open set U such that x ∈ U ⊆ A.

Suppose first that x ∈ A◦ . By definition of interior, there exists r > 0 such that

                                           Br (x) ⊆ A.

Since every open ball is an open set, we may take

                                          U := Br (x).

Then U is open, x ∈ U , and U ⊆ A. Hence there exists an open set U such that x ∈ U ⊆ A.
Conversely, suppose there exists an open set U such that

                                           x ∈ U ⊆ A.

Because U is open and x ∈ U , by definition of openness there exists r > 0 such that

                                           Br (x) ⊆ U.

Since U ⊆ A, it follows that
                                           Br (x) ⊆ A.
Therefore, by definition of interior, we have

                                             x ∈ A◦ .

This proves the equivalence.

Problem 6
Let (X, d) be a metric space and let A be a nonempty subset of X. Prove that A is open if
and only if there exists a set I, positive numbers ri > 0, and points ai ∈ X so that
                                             [
                                         A=    Bri (ai ).

Discussion & Scratch Work

       This problem is really asking me to recognize the structure of open sets in a metric
       space. From the lecture notes, a set is open exactly when every point in it has some
       open ball around it still contained in the set. So the statement here says that open
       sets are precisely unions of open balls.
       To prove this, I need to show both directions. If A is open, then for each point a ∈ A
       I can choose a radius ra > 0 with

                                            Bra (a) ⊆ A.

       Then taking the union of all those balls should recover A. Conversely, if A is already
       given as a union of open balls, then each ball is open by the lecture result, and an
       arbitrary union of open sets is open. So the proof is short: one direction uses the
       definition of openness pointwise, and the other uses the fact that unions of open sets
       are open.

Proof.
We prove that A is open if and only if there exist a set I, positive numbers ri > 0, and points
ai ∈ X such that                            [
                                       A=      Bri (ai ).
                                               i∈I

Suppose first that A is open. Since A is nonempty, for each a ∈ A there exists ra > 0 such
that
                                       Bra (a) ⊆ A.
For each a ∈ A, choose ra > 0 such that

                                           Bra (a) ⊆ A.

Then                                           [
                                         A=          Bra (a).
                                              a∈A

Conversely, suppose there exist a set I, positive numbers ri > 0, and points ai ∈ X such
that                                      [
                                      A=      Bri (ai ).
                                               i∈I

is open.
Therefore A is open if and only if it can be written as a union of open balls.

Appendix: Toolbox
Metrics and Metric Spaces
Definition 1. Let X be a nonempty set. A metric on X is a function d : X × X → R such
that

(1) d(x, y) ≥ 0 for all x, y ∈ X.
(2) d(x, y) = 0 if and only if x = y.
(3) d(x, y) = d(y, x) for all x, y ∈ X.
(4) d(x, z) ≤ d(x, y) + d(y, z) for all x, y, z ∈ X.

Definition 2. If d is a metric on X, then (X, d) is called a metric space.


ℓp Metrics on Rn
Proposition 1 (Hölder’s inequality). Fix 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞, and let 1 ≤ q ≤ ∞ satisfy
                                                   1 1
                                                    + = 1.
                                                   p q

For any (x1 , . . . , xn ), (y1 , . . . , yn ) ∈ Rn we have

                               n                  n
                                                                  !1/p   n
                                                                                       !1/q
                               X                  X                      X
                                                              p                    q
                                     |xk yk | ≤         |xk |                  |yk |          .
                               k=1                k=1                    k=1

                                   1/p
When p = ∞, replace ( nk=1 |xk |p ) by maxk=1,...,n |xk | above.
                     P

Remark. When p = 2 (and so q = 2), we call this the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

Corollary 1 (Minkowski’s inequality). For any 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ and (x1 , . . . , xn ), (y1 , . . . , yn ) ∈
Rn , we have
                  n

                               (P

                   dp (x, y) =
                                maxk=1,...,n |xk − yk | if p = ∞,

is a metric on Rn .

Open Balls and Open Sets
Definition 3. Let (X, d) be a metric space. The open ball (or neighborhood) of radius r > 0
centered at a ∈ X is
                              Br (a) = {x ∈ X : d(a, x) < r}.

Definition 4. Let (X, d) be a metric space and A ⊆ X. We say:

(1) a ∈ X is an interior point of A if there exists r > 0 such that Br (a) ⊆ A.
(2)
                              A◦ = {a ∈ X : a is an interior point of A}
      is the interior of A.
(3) A is open if A◦ = A.

Remark. Note that A◦ ⊆ A for any set A.


Basic Properties of Interior and Open Sets
Proposition 2. Let (X, d) be a metric space and A, B ⊆ X.

(1) If A ⊆ B, then A◦ ⊆ B ◦ .
(2) A◦ ∪ B ◦ ⊆ (A ∪ B)◦ .
(3) A◦ ∩ B ◦ = (A ∩ B)◦ .
(4) (A◦ )◦ = A◦ . In particular, A◦ is an open set.
(5) A◦ is the largest open set contained in A.
(6) A finite intersection of open sets is open.
(7) An arbitrary union of open sets is open.

Remark. An arbitrary intersection of open sets need not be open.

Problem 1. Consider the metric space (R, d) with the usual metric d(x, y) = |x − y|. For
each of the following sets A ⊆ R, find its closure, isolated points, and limit points. Prove
your answer.

 (a) A = [0, 1)
 (b) A = Z
 (c) A = {x ∈ Q : 0 < x < 1}

Problem 2. Let (X, d) be a metric space, let A be a nonempty subset of X, and let U be
an open subset of X.

 (a) Prove that U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.
 (b) Prove that U ∩ A = U ∩ A. (Hint: Use part (a) and properties of closure from lecture.)
 (c) Conclude that if U ∩ A = ∅, then U ∩ A = ∅.

Problem 3. Recall that d2 is a metric on R2 . Consider the subspace

                                    Y = {(x, 0) : x ∈ R}

with the induced metric dY .

 (a) Provide an example of a set A ⊆ Y where A is open in (Y, dY ) but not open in (R2 , d2 ).
 (b) Prove that for any B ⊆ Y , if B is closed in (Y, dY ) then B is closed in (R2 , d2 ).

Problem 4. Let (X, d) be a metric space, {xn }n≥1 a sequence in X, and x ∈ X. Prove that
the sequence {xn }n≥1 converges to x if and only if for every open set U that contains x, we
have xn ∈ U for all but finitely many n ∈ N.

Problem 5. Recall that d2 is a metric on R2 . Suppose that xn = (xn1 , xn2 ) is a sequence of
points in R2 .

 (a) Prove that |xn1 − x1 | ≤ d2 (xn , x) for any x = (x1 , x2 ) ∈ R2 .
 (b) Prove that the sequence {xn }n≥1 converges in (R2 , d2 ) if and only if the sequences
     {xn1 }n≥1 and {xn2 }n≥1 of real numbers both converge.

Problem 6. Let (X, d) be a metric space and {xn }n≥1 a sequence in X. Prove that if
{xn }n≥1 converges to x ∈ X, then for any y ∈ X, the sequence {d(xn , y)}n≥1 of real numbers
converges to d(x, y).

Problem 1
Consider the metric space (R, d) with the usual metric d(x, y) = |x − y|. For each of the
following sets A ⊆ R, find its closure, isolated points, and limit points. Prove your answer.

(a) A = [0, 1)
(b) A = Z
(c) A = {x ∈ Q : 0 < x < 1}

Discussion & Scratch Work

     For this problem, the main job is to identify the adherent points of each set, because
     once I know those, I know the closure. By definition,

                               x∈A     ⇐⇒        ∀r > 0, Br (x) ∩ A ̸= ∅.

     So for each set, I first ask: which points can I never separate from A by a small ball?
     Then I classify the points of A themselves. A point of A is isolated if some small ball
     meets A only at that point, and it is a limit point if every ball contains another point
     of A. So the proof pattern is:

     (1) identify all adherent points, hence determine A;
     (2) check whether points of A can be isolated;
     (3) determine the limit points using the definition directly.

     For [0, 1), I expect the closure to be [0, 1]: the interval contributes all of its own points,
     and the endpoint 1 is still touched by points of A. I do not expect isolated points,
     since points of the interval are never alone in a small ball.
     For Z, I expect the closure to be exactly Z, because non-integers can be separated from
     the integers by a small ball. I also expect every integer to be isolated, since a ball of
     radius less than 21 contains no other integers.
     For Q ∩ (0, 1), I expect the closure to be [0, 1] again, but for a different reason: density
     of Q in R means every ball around a point of [0, 1] contains rationals from (0, 1). I do
     not expect isolated points, because rational points in (0, 1) always have other rational
     points arbitrarily close by.

Proof.
We work in (R, d) with d(x, y) = |x − y|.
(a) Let A = [0, 1). I claim that

                 A = [0, 1],      A′ = [0, 1],          and A has no isolated points.

                                           x ∈ Br (x) ∩ A,

so 1 is also adherent to A. Thus
                                         [0, 1] ⊆ A.

Problem 2
Let (X, d) be a metric space, let A be a nonempty subset of X, and let U be an open subset
of X.

(a) Prove that U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.
(b) Prove that U ∩ A = U ∩ A. (Hint: Use part (a) and properties of closure from lecture.)
(c) Conclude that if U ∩ A = ∅, then U ∩ A = ∅.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     For this problem, I want to use the definition of closure directly and keep the inclusions
     separate. In part (a), if I start with a point in U ∩ A, then I know two things: the
     point lies in the open set U , and every ball around it meets A. Since U is open, I can
     first choose a small ball staying inside U . Then, because the point is in A, that smaller
     ball must still meet A. That gives a point in U ∩ A, which is exactly what I need to
     prove the point lies in U ∩ A.
     For part (b), I use both part (a) and the closure facts from lecture. Part (a) already
     gives one inclusion once I replace U by U , since U is closed and contains U . For the
     other inclusion, I use monotonicity of closure: because U ∩ A ⊆ U and U ∩ A ⊆ A, its
     closure must lie inside both U and A.
     Part (c) is then a short consequence of part (b). If U ∩ A = ∅, then its closure is also
     empty, so part (b) forces U ∩ A = ∅. Since U ⊆ U , I immediately get U ∩ A = ∅.

Proof.
(a) Let x ∈ U ∩ A. We show that x ∈ U ∩ A.
Since x ∈ U and U is open, there exists r0 > 0 such that

                                           Br0 (x) ⊆ U.

Now let r > 0 be arbitrary, and set

                                      ρ := min{r, r0 } > 0.

Because x ∈ A, every open ball around x meets A, so

                                         Bρ (x) ∩ A ̸= ∅.

Choose y ∈ Bρ (x) ∩ A. Since ρ ≤ r0 , we have

                                         y ∈ Br0 (x) ⊆ U,

and since y ∈ A, it follows that
                                           y ∈ U ∩ A.


                                                 5
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                             Homework 9


Also, because ρ ≤ r, we have y ∈ Br (x). Thus

                                    Br (x) ∩ (U ∩ A) ̸= ∅.

Since this holds for every r > 0, the point x is adherent to U ∩ A. Therefore

                                          x ∈ U ∩ A.

Hence
                                       U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.

(b) We prove that
                                       U ∩ A = U ∩ A.

First, since U is open, part (a) applied to the set A and the open set U gives

                                       U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.

Because U ⊆ U , we certainly have

                                       U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.

To prove the desired equality, it is cleaner to establish both inclusions directly.
Since
                             U ∩A⊆U          and       U ∩ A ⊆ A,
monotonicity of closure gives

                             U ∩A⊆U          and       U ∩ A ⊆ A.

Therefore
                                       U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.

For the reverse inclusion, let x ∈ U ∩ A. Since U is open and x ∈ U , every ball around x
meets U ; indeed, if some ball Br (x) were disjoint from U , then x would not be adherent to
U . Now apply part (a) with the set A replaced by A and the open set U : because x ∈ A
and every sufficiently small ball around x meets U , the argument from part (a) shows that
every ball around x meets U ∩ A. Hence

                                          x ∈ U ∩ A.

So
                                       U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A.
Combining the two inclusions gives

                                       U ∩ A = U ∩ A.

(c) Suppose that
                                          U ∩ A = ∅.

                                               6
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)   Homework 9


Then
                              U ∩ A = ∅ = ∅.
By part (b),
                                U ∩ A = ∅.
Since U ⊆ U , we obtain
                            U ∩ A ⊆ U ∩ A = ∅.
Therefore
                                U ∩ A = ∅.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                                Homework 9


Problem 3
Recall that d2 is a metric on R2 . Consider the subspace

                                       Y = {(x, 0) : x ∈ R}

with the induced metric dY .

(a) Provide an example of a set A ⊆ Y where A is open in (Y, dY ) but not open in (R2 , d2 ).
(b) Prove that for any B ⊆ Y , if B is closed in (Y, dY ) then B is closed in (R2 , d2 ).

Discussion & Scratch Work

      For part (a), I just need one example of a subset of Y that is open in the subspace
      metric but not open in all of R2 . Since

                                        Y = {(x, 0) : x ∈ R}

      is the x-axis, a natural choice is an open interval sitting inside that line, such as

                                      A = {(x, 0) : 0 < x < 1}.

      It should be open in Y because, inside the line, every point has a small ball that stays
      on the line and inside the interval. But it should fail to be open in R2 because every
      ordinary open ball in the plane contains points off the x-axis, and those points are not
      in A.
      For part (b), I want to prove a closed-set statement for a subspace. The key fact from
      lecture is that a set is closed if and only if it contains the limits of all convergent se-
      quences in it, and also that convergence in the subspace metric agrees with convergence
      in the ambient space because the induced metric is just the same distance restricted
      to Y . So the cleanest proof is sequential: take a sequence in B ⊆ Y that converges in
      R2 , notice it is automatically a sequence in the subspace Y with the same limit, and
      then use that B is closed in Y to conclude the limit lies in B. That shows B is closed
      in R2 .

Proof.
(a) Let
                                  A := {(x, 0) : 0 < x < 1} ⊆ Y.
We claim that A is open in (Y, dY ) but not open in (R2 , d2 ).
First, let (x, 0) ∈ A, so 0 < x < 1. Set
                                            1
                                     r :=     min{x, 1 − x} > 0.
                                            2
If (y, 0) ∈ Y and dY ((x, 0), (y, 0)) < r, then since dY is induced by d2 ,

                                  |y − x| = dY ((x, 0), (y, 0)) < r.

                                                  8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                         Homework 9


Hence
                                  0 < x − r < y < x + r < 1,
so (y, 0) ∈ A. Therefore
                                        BrY ((x, 0)) ⊆ A,
and A is open in (Y, dY ).
Now we show that A is not open in (R2 , d2 ). Let (x, 0) ∈ A and let r > 0. Then the point

                                               (x, r/2)

satisfies
                                 d2 ((x, 0), (x, r/2)) = r/2 < r,
                R2
so (x, r/2) ∈ Br ((x, 0)). But (x, r/2) ∈
                                        / A since its second coordinate is not 0. Thus no
              2
open ball in R centered at a point of A is contained in A, and therefore A is not open in
(R2 , d2 ).
(b) Let B ⊆ Y , and suppose that B is closed in (Y, dY ). We prove that B is closed in
(R2 , d2 ).
Let {bn }n≥1 be a sequence in B that converges in (R2 , d2 ) to some point b ∈ R2 . Since every
bn lies in Y , we may write

                               bn = (xn , 0)      for some xn ∈ R.

Because
                                          d2 (bn , b) → 0,
the second coordinates of bn converge to the second coordinate of b. But the second coordi-
nate of every bn is 0, so the second coordinate of b must also be 0. Hence

                                               b ∈ Y.

Now, for points of Y , the induced metric is just the restriction of d2 , so

                                   dY (bn , b) = d2 (bn , b) → 0.

Thus the sequence {bn } converges to b in the subspace (Y, dY ). Since B is closed in (Y, dY )
and every term of the sequence lies in B, it follows that

                                               b ∈ B.

Therefore every sequence in B that converges in (R2 , d2 ) has its limit in B. Hence B is closed
in (R2 , d2 ).

Problem 4
Let (X, d) be a metric space, {xn }n≥1 a sequence in X, and x ∈ X. Prove that the sequence
{xn }n≥1 converges to x if and only if for every open set U that contains x, we have xn ∈ U
for all but finitely many n ∈ N.
Discussion & Scratch Work

      This problem is asking me to connect the usual ε-definition of convergence with the
      open-set language from metric spaces. The statement “for all but finitely many n”
      means that eventually every term of the sequence lies in the set under discussion. So
      I want to show that saying
                                          d(xn , x) < ε
      for all sufficiently large n is equivalent to saying that, for every open set U containing
      x, all sufficiently large terms xn lie in U .
      So the proof should go in two directions. If I assume xn → x, then given an open set U
      containing x, openness gives me some ball Br (x) ⊆ U , and convergence tells me that
      eventually xn ∈ Br (x), hence eventually xn ∈ U . Conversely, if I assume the open-set
      condition, then I test it on the particular open set U = Bε (x). Since every open ball
      is open and contains x, the hypothesis gives exactly the usual convergence condition.

Proof.
We prove that

      xn → x      ⇐⇒      for every open set U ∋ x, xn ∈ U for all but finitely many n.

Suppose first that xn → x. Let U be an open set containing x. Since U is open and x ∈ U ,
there exists r > 0 such that
                                      Br (x) ⊆ U.
Because xn → x, there exists N ∈ N such that

                                  d(xn , x) < r         for all n ≥ N.

Equivalently,
                                  xn ∈ Br (x)        for all n ≥ N.
Since Br (x) ⊆ U , it follows that

                                     xn ∈ U        for all n ≥ N.

Hence xn ∈ U for all but finitely many n.
Conversely, suppose that for every open set U containing x, we have xn ∈ U for all but
finitely many n. Let ε > 0. The open ball

                                                  Bε (x)


                                                   10
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)             Homework 9


is an open set containing x. Therefore there exists N ∈ N such that

                               xn ∈ Bε (x)      for all n ≥ N.

By definition of the ball, this means

                               d(xn , x) < ε        for all n ≥ N.

Since ε > 0 was arbitrary, it follows that xn → x.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                              Homework 9


Problem 5
Recall that d2 is a metric on R2 . Suppose that xn = (xn1 , xn2 ) is a sequence of points in R2 .

(a) Prove that |xn1 − x1 | ≤ d2 (xn , x) for any x = (x1 , x2 ) ∈ R2 .
(b) Prove that the sequence {xn }n≥1 converges in (R2 , d2 ) if and only if the sequences
    {xn1 }n≥1 and {xn2 }n≥1 of real numbers both converge.

Discussion & Scratch Work

      For part (a), the key idea is that in R2 with the Euclidean metric, the distance between
      two points controls the distance between each individual coordinate. So if xn → x in
      d2 , then the first coordinates should automatically converge as real numbers. The proof
      is just an inequality: I compare |xn1 − x1 | to the full Euclidean distance.
      For part (b), I want to prove the usual coordinatewise characterization of convergence
      in R2 . One direction should follow from part (a): if the vectors converge, then each
      coordinate converges. For the other direction, if both coordinate sequences converge,
      then I use the definition of d2 and estimate
                                           q
                                    n
                               d2 (x , x) = (xn1 − x1 )2 + (xn2 − x2 )2

      by making each coordinate difference small. So the proof is just two short implications,
      both using the definition of the Euclidean metric.

Proof.
(a) Let xn = (xn1 , xn2 ) and x = (x1 , x2 ) be points in R2 . Then by definition of d2 ,
                                             p

Since (xn2 − x2 )2 ≥ 0, we have

                              (xn1 − x1 )2 ≤ (xn1 − x1 )2 + (xn2 − x2 )2 .

Taking square roots gives
                                       |xn1 − x1 | ≤ d2 (xn , x).

(b) We prove that {xn }n≥1 converges in (R2 , d2 ) if and only if the real sequences {xn1 }n≥1
and {xn2 }n≥1 both converge.
Suppose first that xn → x = (x1 , x2 ) in (R2 , d2 ). By part (a),

                    |xn1 − x1 | ≤ d2 (xn , x)    and       |xn2 − x2 | ≤ d2 (xn , x)

for all n. Since d2 (xn , x) → 0, it follows that

                            |xn1 − x1 | → 0      and       |xn2 − x2 | → 0.

Problem 6
Let (X, d) be a metric space and {xn }n≥1 a sequence in X. Prove that if {xn }n≥1 converges
to x ∈ X, then for any y ∈ X, the sequence {d(xn , y)}n≥1 of real numbers converges to
d(x, y).
Discussion & Scratch Work

     Here,
                                                {d(xn , y)}n≥1
     is a sequence of real numbers, so saying it converges to d(x, y) means exactly that

                     ∀ε > 0 ∃N ∈ N s.t. d(xn , y) − d(x, y) < ε            ∀n ≥ N.

     So, to my understanding, I think the problem is asking me to show that if xn → x in the
     metric space (X, d), then the distances from xn to a fixed point y behave continuously
     and converge to the distance from x to y.
     The key idea is to compare d(xn , y) and d(x, y) using the triangle inequality. The
     standard trick is to write two inequalities,

              d(xn , y) ≤ d(xn , x) + d(x, y)       and      d(x, y) ≤ d(x, xn ) + d(xn , y),

     and then combine them to get

                                    d(xn , y) − d(x, y) ≤ d(xn , x).

     Once I have that estimate, the conclusion is immediate because xn → x means d(xn , x) →
     0.

Proof.
Assume that xn → x in (X, d), and fix y ∈ X. We must show that the real sequence
{d(xn , y)}n≥1 converges to d(x, y).
Let n ≥ 1. By the triangle inequality,

                                   d(xn , y) ≤ d(xn , x) + d(x, y).

Rearranging gives
                                   d(xn , y) − d(x, y) ≤ d(xn , x).
Also, by the triangle inequality,

                      d(x, y) ≤ d(x, xn ) + d(xn , y) = d(xn , x) + d(xn , y),

so
                                   d(x, y) − d(xn , y) ≤ d(xn , x).
Combining these two inequalities, we obtain

                         d(xn , y) − d(x, y) ≤ d(xn , x)          for all n ≥ 1.

                                                     14
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)              Homework 9


Now let ε > 0. Since xn → x, there exists N ∈ N such that

                                 d(xn , x) < ε        for all n ≥ N.

Therefore, for all n ≥ N ,

                             d(xn , y) − d(x, y) ≤ d(xn , x) < ε.

This shows that
                                       d(xn , y) → d(x, y)
as a sequence of real numbers.
