Lecture 22
Definition 1. Let (X, d) be a metric space and A ⊆ X. We say:

(1) a ∈ X is an interior point of A if ∃ r > 0 s.t. Br (a) ⊆ A.
(2)
                                 A◦ = {a ∈ X : a is an interior point of A}
      is the interior of A.
(3) A is open if A◦ = A.
Example 1. X = R2 with the metric d2 , and A = [0, 1] × [0, 1].

 • x=     3 1
                 
           ,
          4 2
                     is an interior point of A: B1/4 (x) ⊆ A.
 • y=     1
              
          2
            ,1       is not an interior point of A: ∀r > 0, Br (y) ⊈ A.
 • A◦ = (0, 1) × (0, 1).
 • A is not open in (R2 , d2 ).
Example 2. Let (X, d) be a metric space.

 • ∅ and X are open sets.
 • Br (a) is an open set ∀a ∈ X and ∀r > 0:
   Claim: Br (a) ⊆ (Br (a))◦ .
   Fix x ∈ Br (a), i.e. d(x, a) < r.
   Want: x ∈ (Br (a))◦ , i.e. ∃ s > 0 s.t. Bs (x) ⊆ Br (a). Set

                                            s = r − d(x, a) > 0.

   Then

             y ∈ Bs (x) ⇒ d(y, x) < s ⇒ d(y, a) ≤ d(y, x) + d(x, a) < s + d(x, a) = r.

   So Bs (x) ⊆ Br (a).
Remark. Note that A◦ ⊆ A for any set A:

                          x ∈ A◦ ⇒ ∃ r > 0 s.t. Br (x) ⊆ A ⇒ x ∈ Br (x) ⊆ A

since d(x, x) = 0 < r.
So, to prove that a set is open, it suffices to show A◦ ⊇ A.
Proposition 1. Let (X, d) be a metric space and A, B ⊆ X.

                                                   16
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                      Homework 9


(1) If A ⊆ B, then A◦ ⊆ B ◦ .
(2) A◦ ∪ B ◦ ⊆ (A ∪ B)◦ .
(3) A◦ ∩ B ◦ = (A ∩ B)◦ .
(4) (A◦ )◦ = A◦ . In particular, A◦ is an open set.
(5) A◦ is the largest open set contained in A.
(6) A finite intersection of open sets is open.
(7) An arbitrary union of open sets is open.
Remark. An arbitrary intersection of open sets need not be open. Eg. in (R, | · |),

Lecture 23
Definition 2. Let (X, d) be a metric space. A set A ⊆ X is closed if Ac is open.
Example 3. Let (X, d) be a metric space.

Definition 4. Let (X, dX ) be a metric space and let ∅ ̸= Y ⊆ X. Then dY : Y × Y → R,
                                           dY (x, y) = dX (x, y)
is a metric on Y , called the induced metric on Y . The metric space (Y, dY ) is called a
subspace of (X, dX ).

Problem 1
Let (X, d) be a metric space and let Y ⊆ X. We call the set Y complete if the subspace
Y with the induced metric is complete. Prove that if Y is complete, then Y is closed in X.
(Hint: It suffices to show Y ⊆ Y . Use sequences.)
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The key definitions here come straight from lecture. First, saying that Y ⊆ X is
     complete means that every Cauchy sequence in the subspace (Y, dY ) converges to some
     point of Y , where dY is the induced metric. Since the induced metric is just the
     restriction of d to pairs of points in Y , a sequence in Y is Cauchy in (Y, dY ) exactly
     when it is Cauchy in (X, d).
     The most important theorem from lecture for this problem is the sequential character-
     ization of closure: for a subset A ⊆ X,
              x∈A      ⇐⇒     there exists a sequence {an } ⊆ A such that an → x.
     We also proved that every convergent sequence in a metric space is Cauchy, and that
     limits in a metric space are unique.
     So the strategy is very direct. Since the hint says it suffices to prove Y ⊆ Y , I start
     with an arbitrary point x ∈ Y . By the closure theorem, there is a sequence {yn } ⊆ Y
     with yn → x in X. Because convergent sequences are Cauchy, {yn } is Cauchy. Since
     Y is complete, that same sequence must converge to some point y ∈ Y . But now the
     sequence {yn } converges to both x and y in the metric space X, so uniqueness of limits
     forces x = y. Hence x ∈ Y . Since every point of Y lies in Y , we get Y ⊆ Y , and
     therefore Y is closed.

Proof.
We will show that
                                              Y ⊆ Y.
Since always Y ⊆ Y , this will imply Y = Y , and hence Y is closed in X.
So let x ∈ Y . By the sequential characterization of closure proved in lecture, there exists a
sequence {yn }n≥1 in Y such that
                                     yn → x         in (X, d).
Since every convergent sequence in a metric space is Cauchy, the sequence {yn } is Cauchy
in (X, d). Because each yn lies in Y and the induced metric on Y is just the restriction of d,
it follows that {yn } is also a Cauchy sequence in the subspace (Y, dY ).
Now Y is complete by assumption, so every Cauchy sequence in (Y, dY ) converges to a point
of Y . Therefore there exists some y ∈ Y such that
                                     yn → y         in (Y, dY ).
But convergence in the subspace metric means exactly the same distance condition as con-
vergence in (X, d), so in fact
                                 yn → y     in (X, d).

                                                2
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                           Homework 10


Thus the sequence {yn } converges in (X, d) to both x and y. By uniqueness of limits in
metric spaces, we must have
                                         x = y.
Since y ∈ Y , it follows that x ∈ Y .
Because x ∈ Y was arbitrary, we conclude that

                                        Y ⊆ Y.

Therefore Y is closed in X.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                     Homework 10


Problem 2
Let (X, d) be a metric space. Prove that if A, B ⊆ X are both compact then A ∪ B is
compact.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The main definition from lecture that matters here is the definition of compactness: a
     subset K ⊆ X is compact if every open cover of K has a finite subcover. So for this
     problem, I think I should start with an arbitrary open cover of A ∪ B and try to show
     that only finitely many of those open sets are needed.
     The big thing is that any open cover of A ∪ B automatically gives an open cover of
     A and also an open cover of B, since both sets are contained in A ∪ B. Because A is
     compact, I can extract finitely many sets from the cover that already cover A. Likewise,
     because B is compact, I can extract finitely many sets from the same cover that cover
     B. Then I simply combine those two finite collections. Their union is still finite, and
     together they cover both A and B, hence they cover A ∪ B.
     So the proof template is: begin with an arbitrary open cover of A ∪ B, apply compact-
     ness once to A, apply compactness again to B, and then merge the two finite subcovers
     into one finite subcover of the union.

Proof.
Let {Ui }i∈I be an open cover of A ∪ B. Thus each Ui is open in X and
                                              [
                                      A∪B ⊆      Ui .
                                                    i∈I

Since A ⊆ A ∪ B, it follows that                [
                                           A⊆         Ui .
                                                i∈I

So {Ui }i∈I is an open cover of A. Because A is compact, there exists a finite set JA ⊆ I such
that                                          [
                                        A⊆        Ui .
                                                i∈JA

Similarly, since B ⊆ A ∪ B, we also have
                                                [
                                           B⊆         Ui .
                                                i∈I

So {Ui }i∈I is an open cover of B. Because B is compact, there exists a finite set JB ⊆ I such
that                                          [
                                        B⊆        Ui .
                                                i∈JB

Now set
                                         J := JA ∪ JB .

                                                4
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                              Homework 10


Since JA and JB are finite, the set J is finite. Moreover,
                                             !           !
                                      [            [        [
                         A∪B ⊆            Ui ∪         Ui =   Ui .
                                     i∈JA          i∈JB     i∈J


Thus {Ui }i∈J is a finite subcover of A ∪ B.
Since the open cover {Ui }i∈I was arbitrary, we conclude that A ∪ B is compact.

Problem 3
Let (X, d) be a metric space.

(a) Prove that if A ⊆ B ⊆ X, A is closed in X, and B is compact, then A is compact.
    (Hint: Given an open cover of A, we can add Ac to get an open cover of B.)
(b) Prove that if C, D ⊆ X are both compact, then C ∩ D is compact. (Hint: Use (a).)

Discussion & Scratch Work

      Both parts of this problem seem like applications of the open-cover definition of com-
      pactness, together with one of the closure results from lecture. For part (a), we are
      given that A ⊆ B ⊆ X, that A is closed in X, and that B is compact. Since com-
      pactness is about open covers, I should begin with an arbitrary open cover of A and
      try to turn it into an open cover of B. The hint tells me exactly how: because A is
      closed, its complement Ac is open, so if I add Ac to my cover of A, then every point
      of B is covered either because it lies in A or because it lies in B \ A ⊆ Ac . Then
      compactness of B gives a finite subcover, and when I restrict back to A, the set Ac
      becomes unnecessary.
      For part (b), I want to prove that the intersection of two compact sets is compact.
      The cleanest way to use part (a) is to observe that if C and D are compact, then by
      the theorem from lecture each compact set is closed. In particular, C is closed in X,
      and of course C ∩ D ⊆ D. Since D is compact, part (a) applies with A = C ∩ D and
      B = D. That immediately gives compactness of C ∩ D.

Proof.
(a) Let {Ui }i∈I be an arbitrary open cover of A, where each Ui is open in X and
                                              [
                                         A⊆      Ui .
                                                    i∈I

Since A is closed in X, the complement Ac is open in X. I claim that

                                         {Ui }i∈I ∪ {Ac }

is an open cover of B.
Indeed, let x ∈ B. If x ∈ A, then since {Ui }i∈I covers A, we have x ∈ Ui for some i ∈ I. If
x∈/ A, then x ∈ Ac . In either case, x lies in one of the sets from {Ui }i∈I ∪ {Ac }. Hence
                                                  !
                                             [
                                     B⊆         Ui ∪ Ac .
                                              i∈I

So this is an open cover of B.
Because B is compact, there exist finitely many indices i1 , . . . , in ∈ I such that

                                   B ⊆ Ui1 ∪ · · · ∪ Uin ∪ Ac .

                                                6
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                       Homework 10


Now intersect both sides with A. Since A ⊆ B, we obtain

                                 A ⊆ A ∩ (Ui1 ∪ · · · ∪ Uin ∪ Ac ) .

Distributing the intersection gives

                           A ⊆ (A ∩ Ui1 ) ∪ · · · ∪ (A ∩ Uin ) ∪ (A ∩ Ac ).

But A ∩ Ac = ∅, so
                                 A ⊆ (A ∩ Ui1 ) ∪ · · · ∪ (A ∩ Uin ).
Since each A ∩ Uik ⊆ Uik , it follows that

                                        A ⊆ Ui1 ∪ · · · ∪ Uin .

Thus {Ui1 , . . . , Uin } is a finite subcover of A. Since the original open cover of A was arbitrary,
we conclude that A is compact.
(b) Suppose that C, D ⊆ X are both compact. By the theorem from lecture, every compact
subset of a metric space is closed. Therefore C is closed in X.
Now note that
                                         C ∩ D ⊆ D ⊆ X.
Since C is closed in X, the set C ∩ D is also closed in D; equivalently, we may simply apply
part (a) with
                                  A = C ∩ D,        B = D.
Indeed, A ⊆ B, the set A = C ∩ D is closed in X as the intersection of two closed sets, and
B = D is compact. Therefore, by part (a), the set C ∩ D is compact.

Problem 4
Let (X, d) be a metric space and let Y ⊆ X. Prove that if Y is compact, then Y is complete.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The main definition here is the definition of completeness: a metric space is complete
     if every Cauchy sequence converges in that space. Since the problem assumes that
     Y ⊆ X is compact, the idea is to start with an arbitrary Cauchy sequence in Y and
     show that it must converge to a point of Y .
     The theorem from lecture that does the heavy lifting is the sequential compactness
     result: if a set is compact, then every sequence in that set has a subsequence that
     converges in the set. We also know from lecture that every convergent sequence is
     Cauchy, and in any metric space a Cauchy sequence can have at most one possible
     limit.
     So the proof template is very standard. I take a Cauchy sequence {yn } in Y . Because
     Y is compact, the sequence has a subsequence {ynk } that converges to some point
     y ∈ Y . Then I use the fact that the whole sequence is Cauchy to show that actually
     the entire sequence must converge to that same point y. That will prove that every
     Cauchy sequence in Y converges in Y , which is exactly what it means for Y to be
     complete.

Proof.
To prove that Y is complete, let {yn }n≥1 be a Cauchy sequence in the subspace (Y, dY ).
Since the induced metric dY is just the restriction of d to Y , we may regard {yn } simply as
a Cauchy sequence of points in Y .
Because Y is compact, every sequence in Y has a subsequence that converges to a point of
Y . Therefore there exists a subsequence {ynk }k≥1 and a point y ∈ Y such that

                                                ynk → y.

We claim that in fact the whole sequence {yn } converges to y.
Let ε > 0. Since {yn } is Cauchy, there exists N1 ∈ N such that
                                            ε
                            d(yn , ym ) <           for all n, m ≥ N1 .
                                            2
Since ynk → y, there exists N2 ∈ N such that
                                                ε
                              d(ynk , y) <              for all k ≥ N2 .
                                                2
Choose k ≥ N2 large enough so that also nk ≥ N1 . Now let n ≥ N1 . By the triangle
inequality,
                          d(yn , y) ≤ d(yn , ynk ) + d(ynk , y).
Since n ≥ N1 and nk ≥ N1 , the Cauchy condition gives
                                                      ε
                                        d(yn , ynk ) < ,
                                                      2
                                                    8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                             Homework 10


and since k ≥ N2 , the convergence of the subsequence gives
                                                   ε
                                       d(ynk , y) < .
                                                   2
Therefore
                                               ε ε
                                   d(yn , y) <   + = ε.
                                               2 2
Since this holds for all n ≥ N1 , we conclude that

                                          yn → y.

Because y ∈ Y , the sequence {yn } converges in Y .
Thus every Cauchy sequence in Y converges to a point of Y , so Y is complete.

Problem 1
Consider the metric space (R, d) with the usual metric d(x, y) = |x − y|. Note that the set
Q is not an interval, and so from lecture we know that it must be disconnected.

(a) Find two sets A, B ⊆ R that are nonempty and separated so that Q = A ∪ B, and prove
    your answer.
(b) Find a set ∅ ̸= C ⊊ Q that is both open and closed in Q, and prove your answer.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     The problem already gives an important fact from lecture: in (R, | · |), every interval is
     connected, so if a subset of R is not an interval, then it cannot be connected. Lecture 29
     proves that every interval in R is connected, and the contrapositive is exactly the idea
     being invoked here: since Q is not an interval, it must be disconnected. In Lectures 28
     and 29, disconnected means that the set can be written as the union of two nonempty
     separated sets, where “separated” means

                               A∩B =∅          and      A ∩ B = ∅.

     This tells me what kind of sets I should try to build.
     The easiest way to split Q is to cut at an irrational
                                                       √ number, because that creates a
     genuine gap inside Q. The most natural choice is 2. So I define
                                     √                   √
                         A := (−∞, 2) ∩ Q,         B := ( 2, ∞) ∩ Q.

     These are clearly nonempty, disjoint, and their union is all of Q. The real work is to
     prove they are separated. The key observation is that, as subsets of R, their closures
     are                                   √              √
                                A = (−∞, 2],         B = [ 2, ∞),
                                                                        √
     so the only possible boundary point where they might “touch” is 2, and that point
     is not rational. Once that is checked, part (a) is done.
     For part (b), the problem is asking for a proper nonempty subset of Q that is both
     open and closed in the subspace metric on Q. In plain English, that means a subset of
     Q that looks like an open set when viewed from inside Q, but whose complement in Q
     also looks open. The same irrational cut works perfectly. If I take
                                                  √
                                       C := (−∞, 2) ∩ Q,
                                                                                      √
     then it should be open in Q because it is the intersection of the open set (−∞, 2) in
     R with Q,√and it should be closed in Q because it is also the intersection of the closed
     set (−∞, 2] in R with Q. That uses exactly the subspace results from lecture: a set
     is open or closed in a subspace precisely when it is the intersection of the subspace
     with a set that is open or closed in the ambient space.




                                                 2
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                  Homework 11


Proof.
(a) Define                      √                       √
                       A := (−∞, 2) ∩ Q,          B := ( 2, ∞) ∩ Q.
Then A, B ⊆ R, and both are nonempty because, for example,

                                     1 ∈ A,       2 ∈ B.

Also,
                                        A ∪ B = Q,
                                             √                √
since every
       √ rational √
                  number is either less than 2 or greater than 2, and no rational number
equals 2 because 2 ∈ / Q.
It remains to show that A and B are separated. First note that
                                  √                      √
                         A = (−∞, 2]       and     B = [ 2, ∞)

in R. Hence                          √     √            
                      A ∩ B = (−∞, 2] ∩ ( 2, ∞) ∩ Q = ∅,
                                      √                          √
because no number can be both at most 2 and strictly greater than 2. Similarly,
                                      √      √
                      A ∩ B = (−∞, 2) ∩ Q ∩ [ 2, ∞) = ∅.

Therefore A and B are separated.
Since A and B are nonempty, separated, and satisfy Q = A ∪ B, this gives a separation of
Q.
(b) Let                                     √
                                   C := (−∞, 2) ∩ Q.
Then C ̸= ∅ since 1 ∈ C, and C ⊊ Q since, for example, 2 ∈ Q \ C.
                                           √
We show that C is open in Q. The set (−∞, 2) is open in R, so by the subspace topology
result from lecture,                         √
                                  C = (−∞, 2) ∩ Q
is open in Q.
                                                √                            √
We next show that C is closed in Q. The set (−∞, 2] is closed in R, and since 2 ∈
                                                                                / Q we
have                            √              √
                          (−∞, 2] ∩ Q = (−∞, 2) ∩ Q = C.
Again by the subspace result from lecture, this implies that C is closed in Q.
Thus C is a nonempty proper subset of Q that is both open and closed in Q.

Problem 2
Let (X, d) be a metric space.

(a) Prove that if F ⊆ X is disconnected and closed in X, then there exist A, B ⊆ X that
    are nonempty, separated, and closed in X so that F = A ∪ B.
(b) Prove that if U ⊆ X is disconnected and open in X, then there exist A, B ⊆ X that
    are nonempty, separated, and open in X so that U = A ∪ B.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     The starting point for both parts is the definition of disconnectedness from Lectures
     28 and 29: a set is disconnected if it can be written as the union of two nonempty
     separated sets. And “separated” means

                                A∩B =∅           and      A ∩ B = ∅.

     So in both parts, I do not need to invent the sets from scratch. The definition of
     disconnectedness already gives me two nonempty separated pieces whose union is the
     set I started with. The actual work is to prove that those pieces inherit the extra
     property I want: closedness in part (a), and openness in part (b).
     For part (a), I start with F ⊆ X disconnected and closed in X. By disconnectedness,
     there exist nonempty separated sets A, B ⊆ F with F = A ∪ B. Since F is closed, I
     want to use it together with the separation conditions to show that A and B are closed
     in X. The key observation is that separation forces

                                F ∩A=A           and      F ∩ B = B.

     Once I have that, each set is an intersection of closed sets, so it is closed.
     For part (b), I start with U ⊆ X disconnected and open in X. Again, disconnectedness
     gives nonempty separated sets A, B ⊆ U with U = A ∪ B. Now I want to prove that
     A and B are open in X. The right way to use separation is to write each piece as an
     intersection of U with the complement of the closure of the other piece:

                                A = U ∩ (B)c ,         B = U ∩ (A)c .

     Since U is open and closures are closed, these are open sets. So the proof in both
     parts is: use disconnectedness first, and then use either closedness or openness of the
     ambient set together with the separation conditions to upgrade the two pieces.

Proof.
(a) Suppose that F ⊆ X is disconnected and closed in X. Since F is disconnected, by
definition there exist sets A, B ⊆ X such that
                              A ̸= ∅,      B ̸= ∅,         F = A ∪ B,
and A and B are separated; that is,
                              A∩B =∅             and      A ∩ B = ∅.

                                                  4
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                            Homework 11


It remains to show that A and B are closed in X.
We claim that
                                       F ∩ A = A.
Indeed, since A ⊆ F and A ⊆ A, we have A ⊆ F ∩ A. Conversely, let x ∈ F ∩ A. Since
F = A ∪ B, either x ∈ A or x ∈ B. But x ∈ B is impossible, because then x ∈ A ∩ B,
contradicting that A and B are separated. Hence x ∈ A. This proves the claim.
Now both F and A are closed in X, so F ∩ A is closed in X. Since F ∩ A = A, it follows
that A is closed in X.
Similarly,
                                       F ∩ B = B,
and therefore B is closed in X.
Thus there exist A, B ⊆ X that are nonempty, separated, and closed in X such that
                                       F = A ∪ B.

(b) Suppose that U ⊆ X is disconnected and open in X. Since U is disconnected, by
definition there exist sets A, B ⊆ X such that
                           A ̸= ∅,     B ̸= ∅,      U = A ∪ B,
and A and B are separated; that is,
                           A∩B =∅           and     A ∩ B = ∅.
It remains to show that A and B are open in X.
We claim that
                                      A = U ∩ (B)c .
First, let x ∈ A. Then x ∈ U since A ⊆ U , and also x ∈
                                                      / B because A ∩ B = ∅. Hence
                                      x ∈ U ∩ (B)c .
So A ⊆ U ∩ (B)c .
Conversely, let x ∈ U ∩ (B)c . Since x ∈ U = A ∪ B, either x ∈ A or x ∈ B. But x ∈ B
is impossible, because every set is contained in its closure, so x ∈ B would imply x ∈ B,
contradicting x ∈ (B)c . Therefore x ∈ A. This proves the claim.
Since U is open and (B)c is open, the set
                                      A = U ∩ (B)c
is open in X.
By the same argument,
                                      B = U ∩ (A)c ,
and therefore B is open in X.
Thus there exist A, B ⊆ X that are nonempty, separated, and open in X such that
                                       U = A ∪ B.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                     Homework 11


Problem 3
Suppose that (X, d) is a metric space such that for any pair of points a, b ∈ X, there exists
a connected set C ⊆ X so that a, b ∈ C. Prove that X is connected.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The statement is saying that any two points of X can be placed inside some connected
     subset of X. Intuitively, that means the space cannot break into two separated pieces,
     because if it did, then a connected set containing one point from each side would be
     forced to lie entirely in one side or entirely in the other, which is impossible. So the
     result I want to prove is really a global connectedness statement coming from the
     existence of enough connected subsets joining pairs of points.
     From Lecture 29, the main result I want is the proposition that if C ⊆ X is connected
     and A, B ⊆ X are separated with C ⊆ A ∪ B, then either C ⊆ A or C ⊆ B. That is
     exactly the theorem that prevents a connected set from straddling a separation. From
     Lectures 28 and 29, I also need the definition of disconnectedness: X is disconnected
     if there exist nonempty separated sets A, B ⊆ X such that X = A ∪ B. And of course,
     X is connected if it is not disconnected.
     So the proof template is contradiction. I assume X is disconnected. Then there exist
     nonempty separated sets A and B whose union is X. I pick one point a ∈ A and one
     point b ∈ B. By hypothesis, there is a connected set C ⊆ X containing both a and b.
     But since C ⊆ X = A ∪ B, the lecture proposition says that either C ⊆ A or C ⊆ B.
     That cannot happen because C contains both a and b. This contradiction shows that
     X must be connected.

Proof.
Suppose, toward a contradiction, that X is disconnected. Then by definition there exist sets
A, B ⊆ X such that
                          A ̸= ∅,      B ̸= ∅,     X = A ∪ B,
and A and B are separated; that is,

                             A∩B =∅             and      A ∩ B = ∅.

Choose points
                                       a ∈ A,         b ∈ B.
By the hypothesis of the problem, there exists a connected set C ⊆ X such that

                                            a, b ∈ C.

Since X = A ∪ B, we have
                                          C ⊆ A ∪ B.
Now apply the proposition from lecture: if a connected set is contained in the union of two
separated sets, then it must lie entirely in one of them. Therefore either

                                   C⊆A          or      C ⊆ B.

                                                 6
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                             Homework 11


But this is impossible, because a ∈ C ∩A and b ∈ C ∩B. If C ⊆ A, then b ∈ A, contradicting
that b ∈ B and A, B are separated (hence disjoint). Similarly, if C ⊆ B, then a ∈ B, again
a contradiction.
Thus our assumption that X is disconnected is false. Therefore X is connected.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                       Homework 11


Problem 4
Let (X, d) be a metric space and A ⊆ X. Prove that if A is connected, then A is connected.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem is really asking me to show that connectedness is preserved when I pass
     from a set to its closure. The relevant definition from Lectures 28 and 29 is that a
     set is disconnected if it can be written as the union of two nonempty separated sets;
     otherwise it is connected. So to prove that A is connected, the natural strategy is
     contradiction: assume that A is disconnected, and then try to use that separation to
     force a separation of A itself.
     The key lecture result I want is the proposition from Lecture 29: if C is connected and
     E, F are separated with C ⊆ E ∪ F , then either C ⊆ E or C ⊆ F . Since A is connected
     and A ⊆ A, this proposition says that if A were split into two separated pieces, then
     all of A would have to lie in just one of them. The remaining idea is then to use the
     fact that points of the closure of A are limits of sequences in A from Lecture 25. If all
     of A lies in one side of the separation, then every limit point of A must also lie on that
     same side, which rules out the other side entirely. That contradiction shows A cannot
     be disconnected.

Proof.
Suppose, toward a contradiction, that A is disconnected. Then by definition there exist
nonempty separated sets E, F ⊆ X such that
                                           A = E ∪ F.
Since A ⊆ A = E ∪ F and A is connected, the proposition from lecture implies that either
                                     A⊆E        or      A ⊆ F.
Without loss of generality, assume that
                                             A ⊆ E.

Now let x ∈ F . Since F ⊆ A, we have x ∈ A. By the sequential characterization of closure
from lecture, there exists a sequence {an } ⊆ A such that
                                             an → x.
But every an ∈ A ⊆ E, so {an } is a sequence in E converging to x. Hence
                                              x ∈ E.
Since also x ∈ F , this shows that
                                           x ∈ E ∩ F,
which contradicts the fact that E and F are separated.
Therefore F = ∅, contradicting that E and F were both nonempty. This contradiction
shows that A is not disconnected. Hence A is connected.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                       Homework 11


Problem 5
Let (X, d) be a metric space and A, B ⊆ X. Prove that if A and B are both connected and
A ∩ B ̸= ∅, then A ∪ B is connected.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This is one of the standard closure properties of connected sets. The main definition
     from Lectures 28 and 29 is that a set is disconnected if it can be written as the union of
     two nonempty separated sets; otherwise it is connected. So the natural proof strategy
     is contradiction: assume that A ∪ B is disconnected, and then analyze what that
     separation would force on the connected sets A and B individually.
     The key lecture result I need is the proposition from Lecture 29 that says: if C is
     connected and E, F are separated with C ⊆ E ∪ F , then either C ⊆ E or C ⊆ F . That
     theorem is exactly designed for this problem. If A ∪ B were disconnected, then I could
     write
                                         A∪B =E∪F
     with E and F nonempty and separated. Since A is connected and lies in E ∪ F , the
     proposition forces all of A to lie in one side. Likewise, all of B must lie in one side.
     Because A ∩ B ̸= ∅, the sets A and B cannot land in different sides. So they must both
     lie in the same side, which would force the other side to be empty. That contradiction
     shows that A ∪ B is connected.

Proof.
Suppose, toward a contradiction, that A ∪ B is disconnected. Then by definition there exist
nonempty separated sets E, F ⊆ X such that

                                         A ∪ B = E ∪ F.

Since A ⊆ A ∪ B = E ∪ F and A is connected, the proposition from lecture implies that
either
                            A⊆E       or     A ⊆ F.
Similarly, since B ⊆ E ∪ F and B is connected, either

                                   B⊆E          or      B ⊆ F.

We claim that A and B must lie in the same side. Indeed, suppose not. Then, after relabeling
if necessary, we would have
                                A⊆E        and      B ⊆ F.
But A ∩ B ̸= ∅, so choose some point x ∈ A ∩ B. Then x ∈ E and x ∈ F , which implies

                                           x ∈ E ∩ F.

This is impossible because separated sets are disjoint. Hence A and B must both be contained
in E, or both be contained in F .

                                                 9
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                             Homework 11


If A ⊆ E and B ⊆ E, then
                                       A ∪ B ⊆ E,
so from A ∪ B = E ∪ F we get F = ∅, contradicting that F is nonempty. The case A ⊆ F
and B ⊆ F similarly implies E = ∅, also a contradiction.
Thus our assumption that A ∪ B is disconnected is false. Therefore A ∪ B is connected.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                                      Homework 11


Problem 6
Let (X, d) be a metric space.

(a) Let A, B, C ⊆ X. Prove that if the sets A and B are separated and the sets A and C
    are separated, then the sets A and B ∪ C are separated.
(b) Prove that if F, G ⊆ X are closed and F ∪ G and F ∩ G are connected, then F is
    connected.

Discussion & Scratch Work

     For part (a), the whole problem is about carefully unpacking the definition of separated
     sets. From lecture, E and F are separated if

                                E∩F =∅         and      E ∩ F = ∅.

     So if I want to prove that A and B ∪ C are separated, I need to check exactly those two
     conditions with B ∪ C in place of one of the sets. The first condition is easy, because

                                A ∩ (B ∪ C) = (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ C),

     and both pieces are empty by hypothesis. For the second condition, the key topological
     fact is
                                      B ∪ C ⊆ B ∪ C.
     Then
                                A ∩ B ∪ C ⊆ (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ C),
     and again both pieces are empty. So part (a) is really just a direct closure computation.
     For part (b), the assumptions are that F and G are closed, that F ∪ G is connected,
     and that F ∩ G is connected. I want to prove that F itself is connected. The natural
     strategy is contradiction: assume F is disconnected. Then by Problem 2(a), because
     F is also closed, I can write
                                          F =A∪B
     where A and B are nonempty, separated, and closed in X. Since F ∩ G is connected
     and sits inside A ∪ B, the connected-set proposition from Lecture 29 says that F ∩ G
     must lie entirely in one side. Without loss of generality, that means

                                           F ∩ G ⊆ A.

     Then G ∩ B = ∅. Since both G and B are closed, that actually makes G and B
     separated. We already know A and B are separated, so by part (a), B is separated
     from A ∪ G. But then
                                 F ∪ G = (A ∪ G) ∪ B
     is a separation of F ∪ G, contradicting connectedness. So F must be connected.




                                                11
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                            Homework 11


Proof.
(a) Suppose that A and B are separated and that A and C are separated. We show that A
and B ∪ C are separated.
First,
                            A ∩ (B ∪ C) = (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ C).
Since A and B are separated, we have A ∩ B = ∅, and since A and C are separated, we have
A ∩ C = ∅. Therefore
                                   A ∩ (B ∪ C) = ∅.

Next, because B ⊆ B ∪ C and C ⊆ B ∪ C, we have

                                     B ∪ C ⊆ B ∪ C.

Hence
                     A ∩ B ∪ C ⊆ A ∩ (B ∪ C) = (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ C).
Again, since A is separated from both B and C, we have

                           A∩B =∅            and       A ∩ C = ∅.

Therefore
                                     A ∩ B ∪ C = ∅.

Thus
                      A ∩ (B ∪ C) = ∅         and      A ∩ B ∪ C = ∅,
so A and B ∪ C are separated.
(b) Suppose, toward a contradiction, that F is disconnected. Since F is also closed in X,
Problem 2(a) implies that there exist sets A, B ⊆ X such that

                           A ̸= ∅,        B ̸= ∅,      F = A ∪ B,

and A and B are separated and closed in X.
Now consider the set F ∩G. Since F ∩G ⊆ F = A∪B and F ∩G is connected by assumption,
the proposition from Lecture 29 implies that either

                            F ∩G⊆A            or    F ∩ G ⊆ B.

Without loss of generality, assume that

                                          F ∩ G ⊆ A.

We claim that G and B are separated. Indeed, since B ⊆ F , we have

                              G ∩ B ⊆ G ∩ F = F ∩ G ⊆ A.

But A and B are separated, hence disjoint, so A ∩ B = ∅. Therefore

                                          G ∩ B = ∅.

                                              12
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026                                            Homework 11


Because G and B are both closed in X, we have G = G and B = B, and so

                     G ∩ B = G ∩ B = ∅,         G ∩ B = G ∩ B = ∅.

Thus G and B are separated.
We already know that A and B are separated. Applying part (a) with the roles of the sets
relabeled, we conclude that B and A ∪ G are separated.
Now
                          F ∪ G = (A ∪ B) ∪ G = (A ∪ G) ∪ B.
Also, B ̸= ∅ by construction, and A ∪ G ̸= ∅ because A ̸= ∅. Since B and A ∪ G are
separated, this shows that F ∪ G is disconnected, contradicting the assumption that F ∪ G
is connected.
Therefore F must be connected.

Problem 1
Let (X, dX ) and (Y, dY ) be metric spaces, f : X → Y , and x0 ∈ X. Prove that f is
continuous at x0 if and only if for any open set V ⊆ Y containing f (x0 ), there is an open
set U ⊆ X containing x0 so that x ∈ U implies f (x) ∈ V .
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem is asking me to prove that two definitions of continuity at a point are
     equivalent. From lecture, I know the standard ε–δ definition: f is continuous at x if
     for every ε > 0 there exists δ > 0 such that

                             dX (x′ , x) < δ =⇒ dY (f (x′ ), f (x)) < ε.

     I also know the open-set formulation: f is continuous at x if for every open set V ⊆ Y
     with f (x) ∈ V , there exists an open set U ⊆ X such that

                                  x∈U         and      f (U ) ⊆ V.

     So the goal is to show these two definitions are equivalent. Since this is an “if and only
     if,” I prove both directions.
     For the forward direction, I assume the ε–δ definition. If V is open and contains f (x),
     then there exists an open ball Bε (f (x)) ⊆ V . Then continuity gives a δ > 0 such that
     f (Bδ (x)) ⊆ Bε (f (x)) ⊆ V . So I take U = Bδ (x).
     For the reverse direction, I assume the open-set condition. Given ε > 0, I consider the
     open ball Bε (f (x)). By hypothesis, there exists an open set U containing x such that
     f (U ) ⊆ Bε (f (x)). Since U is open, there exists δ > 0 such that Bδ (x) ⊆ U . This gives
     the ε–δ condition.

Proof.
(⇒) Assume that f is continuous at x ∈ X. Let V ⊆ Y be open with f (x) ∈ V . Then there
exists ε > 0 such that
                                     Bε (f (x)) ⊆ V.
By continuity at x, there exists δ > 0 such that

                            dX (x′ , x) < δ =⇒ dY (f (x′ ), f (x)) < ε.

Let U := Bδ (x). Then U is open, x ∈ U , and if x′ ∈ U , then f (x′ ) ∈ Bε (f (x)) ⊆ V . Hence

                                            f (U ) ⊆ V.

(⇐) Assume that for every open set V ⊆ Y with f (x) ∈ V , there exists an open set U ⊆ X
such that x ∈ U and f (U ) ⊆ V .
Let ε > 0 and set
                                         V := Bε (f (x)).


                                                 2
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                      Homework 12


Then V is open and contains f (x), so there exists an open set U ⊆ X such that

                             x∈U            and         f (U ) ⊆ Bε (f (x)).

Since U is open and x ∈ U , there exists δ > 0 such that

                                              Bδ (x) ⊆ U.

Now if dX (x′ , x) < δ, then x′ ∈ U , so

                                           f (x′ ) ∈ Bε (f (x)),

which implies
                                       dY (f (x′ ), f (x)) < ε.
Therefore f is continuous at x.

MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                             Homework 12


Problem 2
Let (X, dX ) and (Y, dY ) be metric spaces and f : X → Y a function. Prove that f is
continuous if and only if f (A) ⊆ f (A) for any A ⊆ X.
Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem is asking me to prove an equivalent characterization of continuity using
     closures. I am given a function f : X → Y between metric spaces, and I need to show
     that
                      f is continuous ⇐⇒ f (A) ⊆ f (A) for all A ⊆ X.

     So I need to prove both directions.
     First, I unpack what this statement means. The set A consists of all points x that are
     either in A or are limits of sequences from A. Similarly, f (A) consists of all points in
     Y that are limits of sequences from f (A).
     So the statement f (A) ⊆ f (A) is saying: if x is either in A or is a limit of points in A,
     then f (x) should be either in f (A) or a limit of points in f (A).
     This strongly suggests using the sequential characterization of closure and the fact from
     lecture that:
                         f continuous ⇐⇒ xn → x ⇒ f (xn ) → f (x).

     For the forward direction, I assume f is continuous. Then if x ∈ A, I can find a sequence
     an ∈ A with an → x. Continuity will give f (an ) → f (x), which means f (x) ∈ f (A).
     For the reverse direction, I should not use subsequences. Instead, I will use the open-set
     characterization from Problem 1. So I fix an open set V ⊆ Y and a point x ∈ X such
     that f (x) ∈ V , and I define
                                         A := X \ f −1 (V ).
     If x were in A, then the hypothesis would imply

                                       f (x) ∈ f (A) ⊆ f (A).

     But every point of f (A) lies in Y \ V , so f (A) ⊆ Y \ V because Y \ V is closed. That
     would force f (x) ∈
                       / V , a contradiction. Hence x ∈ / A.
     Since x ∈/ A, there exists an open set U containing x with U ∩ A = ∅. Therefore
     U ⊆ X \ A = f −1 (V ), so f (U ) ⊆ V . This is exactly the open-set characterization of
     continuity.

Proof.
(⇒) Assume that f is continuous. Let A ⊆ X, and let x ∈ A. By the sequential characteri-
zation of closure, there exists a sequence {an } ⊆ A such that

                                              an → x.

Since f is continuous, we have
                                           f (an ) → f (x).

                                                  4
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                     Homework 12


Each f (an ) ∈ f (A), so f (x) is a limit of points in f (A). Hence
                                          f (x) ∈ f (A).
Therefore
                                         f (A) ⊆ f (A).
(⇐) Assume that for all A ⊆ X, we have
                                         f (A) ⊆ f (A).
We show that f is continuous.
Let V ⊆ Y be open, and let x ∈ X satisfy
                                           f (x) ∈ V.
Set
                                       A := X \ f −1 (V ).
We claim that x ∈
                / A. Suppose for contradiction that
                                             x ∈ A.
Then, since x ∈ A, we have f (x) ∈ f (A). By the hypothesis,
                                         f (A) ⊆ f (A),
so
                                          f (x) ∈ f (A).
                                                         / f −1 (V ), we must have
Now if y ∈ f (A), then y = f (a) for some a ∈ A. Since a ∈
                                           f (a) ∈
                                                 / V.
Thus
                                         f (A) ⊆ Y \ V.
Because V is open, the set Y \ V is closed. Therefore
                                         f (A) ⊆ Y \ V.
It follows that
                                         f (x) ∈ Y \ V,
which contradicts f (x) ∈ V .
Hence x ∈/ A. Since A is closed, its complement is open, so there exists an open set U ⊆ X
such that
                              x∈U        and     U ∩ A = ∅.
Therefore
                                     U ⊆ X \ A = f −1 (V ).
So if u ∈ U , then u ∈ f −1 (V ), which means f (u) ∈ V . Hence
                                           f (U ) ⊆ V.
We have shown that for every open set V ⊆ Y containing f (x), there exists an open set
U ⊆ X containing x such that f (U ) ⊆ V . By Problem 1, this means that f is continuous at
x. Since x ∈ X was arbitrary, f is continuous on X.

Problem 3
                                        1
Let f : R → R be the function f (x) = 1+x 2.



(a) Prove that f is continuous.
(b) Find an open set U ⊆ R so that f (U ) is not open.
(c) Find a closed set F ⊆ R so that f (F ) is not closed.
(d) Find a set A ⊆ R so that f (A) ̸= f (A).

Discussion & Scratch Work

     This problem is asking me to analyze a specific function
                                                         1
                                          f (x) =
                                                      1 + x2
     from several perspectives: continuity, behavior of images of open/closed sets, and how
     closure interacts with the function.
     For part (a), I need to prove f is continuous. From Lecture 30, I know I can use the
     sequential definition: f is continuous if xn → x implies f (xn ) → f (x). This is the
     easiest approach here since rational functions behave well under limits.
     For part (b), I need to find an open set U such that f (U ) is not open. So I need
     to recall that a set is not open if it fails to contain an open ball around some point.
     Intuitively, f maps R into (0, 1], and the maximum value 1 is only achieved at x = 0.
     That suggests that images of open sets containing 0 will include 1 but not values larger
     than 1, which creates a boundary point—so the image won’t be open.
     For part (c), I need a closed set F such that f (F ) is not closed. From Lecture 31, con-
     tinuous functions do not necessarily preserve closed sets unless the domain is compact.
     So I should look for a closed set whose image “misses” a limit point.
     For part (d), I need to find a set A where

                                          f (A) ̸= f (A).

     From Problem 2, I know that continuity gives only one inclusion:

                                          f (A) ⊆ f (A).

     So I need an example where this inclusion is strict. This typically happens when limit
     points are “collapsed” under the function, meaning some points in f (A) are not hit by
     f (A).

Proof.
(a) Let {xn } be a sequence in R such that xn → x. Then We use the standard limit laws
from earlier lectures: polynomials and reciprocal functions are continuous where defined.
Then
                                        x2n → x2 ,

                                                  6
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                      Homework 12


so
                                       1 + x2n → 1 + x2 .
Since the function t 7→ 1t is continuous for t ̸= 0, we have

                                          1       1
                                            2
                                              →        .
                                       1 + xn   1 + x2

Thus f (xn ) → f (x), so f is continuous.
(b) Let U = R, which is open. Then

                                        f (U ) = (0, 1] .

This set is not open in R because 1 ∈ f (U ), but there is no open interval around 1 contained
in (0, 1]. Hence f (U ) is not open.
(c) Let F = [0, ∞), which is closed in R. Then

                                        f (F ) = (0, 1] .

As above, (0, 1] is not closed because it does not contain 0, which is a limit point. Hence
f (F ) is not closed.
(d) Let
                                            A = (0, ∞).
Then
                                            A = [0, ∞).
So
                                  f (A) = f ([0, ∞)) = (0, 1].
On the other hand,
                                  f (A) = f ((0, ∞)) = (0, 1),
so
                                         f (A) = [0, 1].
Thus
                                f (A) = (0, 1] ̸= [0, 1] = f (A).

Problem 4
Consider the metric spaces (R, | · |) and (R2 , d2 ).

(a) Prove that the functions f, g : R2 → R given by f (x, y) = x and g(x, y) = y are
    continuous. (Hint: Use sequences.)
(b) Prove that if h : [0, 1] → R2 is a continuous function such that h(0) = (x0 , y0 ), h(1) =
    (x1 , y1 ), and x0 < 0 < x1 , then there exists a point t ∈ (0, 1) so that h(t) lies on the
    y-axis.

Discussion & Scratch Work

      For part (a), the problem explicitly tells me to use sequences, so I should use the sequen-
      tial characterization of continuity from lecture: a function is continuous if whenever
      a sequence converges in the domain, its image converges in the codomain. Here the
      functions are just the coordinate projections

                                 f (x, y) = x        and    g(x, y) = y.

      So if (xn , yn ) → (x, y) in (R2 , d2 ), then by the definition of the Euclidean metric,
                                                    p
                           d2 ((xn , yn ), (x, y)) = (xn − x)2 + (yn − y)2 → 0.

      That immediately forces both xn → x and yn → y, so the coordinate functions should
      be continuous.
      For part (b), the key idea is to compose the given path h : [0, 1] → R2 with the
      projection onto the first coordinate. If I define

                                            φ(t) := f (h(t)),

      then φ : [0, 1] → R is continuous because h is continuous and part (a) says f is
      continuous. Also,
                          φ(0) = x0 < 0     and    φ(1) = x1 > 0.
      So now I can use the Intermediate Value Theorem from calculus on φ. That gives some
      t ∈ (0, 1) with φ(t) = 0, which means the first coordinate of h(t) is 0. In other words,
      h(t) lies on the y-axis.

Proof.
(a) We first prove that f : R2 → R given by f (x, y) = x is continuous. Let {(xn , yn )} be a
sequence in R2 such that
                                    (xn , yn ) → (x, y)
in (R2 , d2 ). Then
                                                   p
                       d2 ((xn , yn ), (x, y)) =    (xn − x)2 + (yn − y)2 → 0.


                                                      8
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                           Homework 12


Since                           p
                   |xn − x| ≤    (xn − x)2 + (yn − y)2 = d2 ((xn , yn ), (x, y)),
it follows by the squeeze theorem that

                                                xn → x.

Hence
                                    f (xn , yn ) = xn → x = f (x, y).
Therefore f is continuous.
The proof for g : R2 → R given by g(x, y) = y is exactly the same. If (xn , yn ) → (x, y), then
                          p
                |yn − y| ≤ (xn − x)2 + (yn − y)2 = d2 ((xn , yn ), (x, y)) → 0,

so
                                                yn → y.
Thus
                                    g(xn , yn ) = yn → y = g(x, y),
and therefore g is continuous.
(b) Let h : [0, 1] → R2 be continuous, with

                     h(0) = (x0 , y0 ),      h(1) = (x1 , y1 ),     x0 < 0 < x1 .

Define
                                φ : [0, 1] → R,        φ(t) := f (h(t)),
where f : R2 → R is the first-coordinate projection from part (a), namely f (x, y) = x.
Since h is continuous and f is continuous, the composition φ = f ◦ h is continuous on [0, 1].
Moreover,
                           φ(0) = f (h(0)) = f (x0 , y0 ) = x0 < 0,
and
                              φ(1) = f (h(1)) = f (x1 , y1 ) = x1 > 0.
By the Intermediate Value Theorem from calculus, which we may assume, there exists some
t ∈ (0, 1) such that
                                      φ(t) = 0.
But φ(t) = f (h(t)) is exactly the first coordinate of h(t). So the first coordinate of h(t) is 0,
which means
                                     h(t) ∈ {(0, y) : y ∈ R},
that is, h(t) lies on the y-axis.

Problem 5
Going forward, you may assume (no proof necessary) any fact about sin x and cos x from
calculus, such as sin, cos : R → [−1, 1] are continuous functions. Prove that the function
f : R2 → R given by
                               f (x, y) = x2 + y 2 + sin(x − y)
has a global minimum. (Hint: Notice that we cannot directly apply the extreme value
theorem here. However, we can apply it to any compact subset of R2 .)
Discussion & Scratch Work

     The goal is to prove that

                                    f (x, y) = x2 + y 2 + sin(x − y)

     has a global minimum on R2 . The hint is important: I cannot apply the Extreme Value
     Theorem directly on all of R2 , because R2 is not compact. So the idea is to first show
     that I only need to look on some compact subset of R2 , and then apply the Extreme
     Value Theorem there.
     First, I note that f is continuous. The functions (x, y) 7→ x2 + y 2 and (x, y) 7→ x − y are
     continuous, and by the problem statement I may assume that sin is continuous. There-
     fore (x, y) 7→ sin(x − y) is continuous, and so f is continuous as a sum of continuous
     functions.
     Next, I want a lower bound that tells me what happens far away from the origin. Since

                                           −1 ≤ sin(x − y) ≤ 1,

     I get
                                           f (x, y) ≥ x2 + y 2 − 1.
     So if (x, y) is far from the origin, then x2 + y 2 is large, and therefore f (x, y) is large as
     well. This is the key coercive-type estimate.
     To use this effectively, I want to compare that lower bound with the value of f at some
     specific point. A convenient choice is
                                                   
                                                1 1
                                              − ,      ,
                                                2 2

     because then
                                                x − y = −1,
     so                           
                            1 1            1 1           1
                         f − ,         =    + + sin(−1) = − sin(1) < 0.
                            2 2            4 4           2
     On the other hand, if x2 + y 2 ≥ 1, then

                                       f (x, y) ≥ x2 + y 2 − 1 ≥ 0.



                                                     10
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                              Homework 12


       So every point outside the closed unit disk has f (x, y) ≥ 0, while the point − 12 , 12
                                                                                               

       inside the closed unit disk gives a negative value. That means a global minimizer
       cannot occur outside the closed unit disk.
       So I restrict to
                                   K = {(x, y) ∈ R2 : x2 + y 2 ≤ 1}.
       This set is closed and bounded in R2 , hence compact. Since f is continuous, the
       Extreme Value Theorem implies that f attains a minimum on K. Because every point
       outside K has value at least 0, while some point in K has value < 0, that minimum
       on K is automatically a global minimum on all of R2 .

Proof.
Define
                           f : R2 → R,      f (x, y) = x2 + y 2 + sin(x − y).

First, f is continuous on R2 . Indeed, (x, y) 7→ x2 + y 2 is continuous, (x, y) 7→ x − y is
continuous, and sin : R → R is continuous, so (x, y) 7→ sin(x − y) is continuous. Therefore
f is continuous as a sum of continuous functions.
Next, since
                                           sin(x − y) ≥ −1
for all (x, y) ∈ R2 , we have

                            f (x, y) = x2 + y 2 + sin(x − y) ≥ x2 + y 2 − 1.

In particular, whenever x2 + y 2 ≥ 1, we get

                                              f (x, y) ≥ 0.

Now consider the point                             
                                                1 1
                                            p= − ,    .
                                                2 2
Then                                    
                                     1 1    1 1           1
                          f (p) = f − ,    = + + sin(−1) = − sin(1).
                                     2 2    4 4           2
Since sin(1) > 12 , it follows that
                                               f (p) < 0.
Also, p lies in the closed unit disk

                                  K := {(x, y) ∈ R2 : x2 + y 2 ≤ 1}.

The set K is closed and bounded in R2 , hence compact. Since f is continuous, the Extreme
Value Theorem implies that there exists some q ∈ K such that

                                    f (q) ≤ f (z)        for all z ∈ K.


                                                    11
MATH 521 — LEC 002, Spring 2026 (MWF 1:20–2:10 — VV B119)                  Homework 12


We claim that q is a global minimizer of f on R2 . Let z = (x, y) ∈ R2 .
If z ∈ K, then by definition of q,
                                        f (q) ≤ f (z).

     / K, then x2 + y 2 > 1, so from the estimate above,
If z ∈

                                          f (z) ≥ 0.

But
                                      f (q) ≤ f (p) < 0,
so again
                                      f (q) < 0 ≤ f (z).

Thus in every case,
                               f (q) ≤ f (z)        for all z ∈ R2 .
Therefore f has a global minimum on R2 .

Problem 6
Let X and Y be metric spaces and f : X → Y a function. Prove that if X is compact and f
is continuous and bijective, then the inverse function f −1 : Y → X is also continuous. (Hint:
It suffices to show that the preimage of any closed subset of X under f −1 is closed in Y .)
Discussion & Scratch Work

      The goal is to prove that f −1 : Y → X is continuous. The hint is telling me exactly
      which continuity criterion to use: it is enough to show that for every closed set C ⊆ X,
      the preimage of C under f −1 is closed in Y .
      So I should start with an arbitrary closed set C ⊆ X and understand what

                                               (f −1 )−1 (C)

      actually is. Since f is bijective, this set is just

                                                  f (C).

      So the real task is to prove that f (C) is closed in Y whenever C is closed in X.
      Now the compactness assumption is what makes this work. Since X is compact and
      C is closed in X, I know from lecture that C is compact. Since f is continuous, the
      image of a compact set under f is compact. And since Y is a metric space, compact
      subsets of Y are closed. So the chain is:

            C closed in X =⇒ C compact =⇒ f (C) compact =⇒ f (C) closed in Y.

      That is exactly what I need to show that f −1 is continuous.

Proof.
We prove that f −1 : Y → X is continuous by using the closed-set criterion.
Let C ⊆ X be closed. Since X is compact and C is closed in X, it follows that C is compact.
Because f : X → Y is continuous, the image of a compact set under f is compact. Hence

                                                  f (C)

is compact in Y .
Now Y is a metric space, and in a metric space every compact set is closed. Therefore f (C)
is closed in Y .
Since f is bijective, we have
                                         (f −1 )−1 (C) = f (C).
Thus (f −1 )−1 (C) is closed in Y for every closed set C ⊆ X.
Therefore, by the closed-set criterion for continuity, f −1 is continuous.
